(which must contain all these intersections). We denote

E(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2})=n^{-1}([\![{{\mathsf{x}}_{1}-{\mathsf{t}}_{1},{\mathsf{x}}_{1}}]\!]\triangle[\![{{\mathsf{x}}_{2}-{\mathsf{t}}_{2}+1,{\mathsf{x}}_{2}-1}]\!]),

which is the set of poles of
\mathsf{D}^{\prime}_{1}-\mathsf{D}^{\prime}_{2}
(
\triangle
denotes the symmetric difference).
Similar to Lemma
5.12
, we have that for any
z\not\in E(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2})
,

(5.46)

\mathsf{D}^{\prime}_{1}(z)-\mathsf{D}^{\prime}_{2}(z)\lesssim\frac{1}{\min_{x\in E(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2})}n|z-x|}+\log n.

The zero set of
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2})
can be described as follows.

Lemma 5.22
.

The set
\{z\in\mathbb{H}\cup\mathbb{R}\setminus E(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2}):\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(z)-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z))=0\}
contains the following parts:

(1)

The connected component of
\mathbb{R}\setminus E(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2})
containing
z_{c}
;

(2)

If either (i)
{\mathsf{x}}_{1}-{\mathsf{t}}_{1}<{\mathsf{x}}_{2}-{\mathsf{t}}_{2}+1
and
{\mathsf{x}}_{1}>{\mathsf{x}}_{2}-1
, or (ii)
{\mathsf{x}}_{1}-{\mathsf{t}}_{1}>{\mathsf{x}}_{2}-{\mathsf{t}}_{2}+1
and
{\mathsf{x}}_{1}<{\mathsf{x}}_{2}-1
, the set
\{z\in\mathbb{H}\cup\mathbb{R}\setminus E(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2}):\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(z)-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z))=0\}
also contains a smooth and self-avoiding curve
\ell_{d}
from the connected component of
\mathbb{R}\setminus E(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2})
containing
z_{c}
to
\infty
. Except for the starting point,
\ell_{d}
is contained in
\mathbb{H}
.
Moreover,
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2})
is strictly monotone along
\ell_{d}
, and
\ell_{d}
is disjoint from the
n^{-3}
neighborhood of
E(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2})
.

For convenience, if neither (i) nor (ii) in (2) holds, we denote
\ell_{d}=\emptyset
.
This lemma can be proved by analyzing the critical points of
\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2}
and using arguments similar to the proofs of Lemmas
5.17
and
5.18
. We omit the details here.

5.6.2.
Curve discretization

Choose
\xi=n^{-100}
, we denote

\Lambda=2\xi\mathbb{Z}+2\mathbf{i}\xi\mathbb{Z},\quad\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda+(1+\mathbf{i})\xi.

For any discrete interval
I
(i.e.,
I
is a set of consecutive integers) and
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, we call
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in I}
a
\Lambda
-path
(resp.
\Lambda^{\prime}
-path
) if the followings hold: all these
z_{i}
are different points in
\Lambda
(resp.
\Lambda^{\prime}
), and every pair
z_{i}
and
z_{i+1}
are nearest neighbors on the lattice
\Lambda
(resp.
\Lambda^{\prime}
). The curve obtained by connecting every pair of points
z_{i}
and
z_{i+1}
using a line segment is called the corresponding
\Lambda
-curve
(resp.
\Lambda^{\prime}
-curve
).
We first discretize (i.e., approximate)
\ell_{2,2}
by a
\Lambda^{\prime}
-curve.

Lemma 5.23
.

There exists a
\Lambda^{\prime}
-curve
\ell^{*}_{2,2}
from a point in the intersection of
\Lambda^{\prime}
and the
2\xi
-neighborhood of
z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\theta_{2}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}
to
\infty
, such that
(1) for any
z\in\Lambda^{\prime}\cap\ell^{*}_{2,2}
, we have
(z+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi])\cap\ell_{2,2}\neq\emptyset
, and (2)
\ell^{*}_{2,2}
is disjoint from the
n^{-3}/2
-neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let
z_{*}
be a vertex in
\Lambda^{\prime}\cap(z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\theta_{2}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi])
.
Denote

\Lambda^{\prime}_{2,2}:=\{z\in U\cap\Lambda^{\prime}:(z+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi])\cap\ell_{2,2}\neq\emptyset\}.

If we cannot find a
\Lambda^{\prime}
-curve satisfying (1), then the set of points that are connected to
z_{*}
through a
\Lambda^{\prime}
-curve contained in
\Lambda^{\prime}_{2,2}
must be finite. Then, we can find a sequence of numbers
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\in\Lambda^{\prime}\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}_{2,2}
, such that
z_{k}=z_{1}
,
|z_{i+1}-z_{i}|\leq 2\sqrt{2}\xi
for each
i
, and
z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\theta_{2}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}
is enclosed by
[z_{1}\to\cdots\to z_{k}]
. In this case,
\ell_{2,2}
must intersect
[z_{1}\to\cdots\to z_{k}]
, contradicting the fact that
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}\not\in\Lambda^{\prime}_{2,2}
.
The statement (2) follows immediately from (1) and Lemma
5.18
.
∎

For
\ell_{d}
from Lemma
5.22
, we have a similar discretization. Its proof is similar to that of Lemma
5.23
, so we omit the details.

Lemma 5.24
.

For any two points
z_{1},z_{2}\in\ell_{d}
, there exists a
\Lambda^{\prime}
-curve connecting two
\Lambda^{\prime}
points lying in the
2\xi
-neighborhoods of
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, respectively, such that
this
\Lambda^{\prime}
-curve is in the
2\xi
-neighborhood of the part of
\ell_{d}
between
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
,
and is disjoint from the
n^{-3}/2
-neighborhood of
E(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2})
.

We next state the discretizations for
\ell_{1,1}
and
\ell_{1,3}
, which use the lattice
\Lambda
instead of
\Lambda^{\prime}
, so that their possible intersections with
\ell^{*}_{2,2}
are points in the lattice
\Lambda+\{\xi,\mathbf{i}\xi\}
.
We also require these points to be close to
\ell_{d}
, in order to handle the residues resulting from these intersections. Therefore, the discretizations for
\ell_{1,1}
and
\ell_{1,3}
are slightly more delicate than that for
\ell_{2,2}
.

Lemma 5.25
.

There exists a
\Lambda
-curve
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
(resp.
\ell^{*}_{1,3}
) from a point in the intersection of
\Lambda
and the
5\xi
-neighborhood of
z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\vartheta_{1}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}
(resp.
z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\vartheta_{3}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}
) to
P_{1,-}
(resp.
P_{1,+}
), such that (1) it is contained in the
5\xi
neighborhood of
\ell_{1,1}
(resp.
\ell_{1,3}
), (2) it is disjoint from the
n^{-3}/2
-neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}\setminus P_{1,-}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}\setminus P_{1,+}
), and (3) the set
\ell^{*}_{1,1}\cap\ell^{*}_{2,2}
(resp.
\ell^{*}_{1,3}\cap\ell^{*}_{2,2}
) is contained in the
3\xi
-neighborhood of
\ell_{d}
.

Proof.

We note that the set
(\mathbb{H}\cup\mathbb{R})\setminus(\ell_{1,1}\cup[z_{c}\to z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\vartheta_{1}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}])
contains two connected components, and we denote the bounded one by
U_{1,-}
, and the unbounded one plus the boundary
\ell_{1,1}\cup[z_{c}\to z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\vartheta_{1}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}]
by
U_{1,+}
.
Let
\Lambda^{\prime}_{-}
denote the set of
z\in\mathbb{H}\cap\Lambda^{\prime}
, such that either

(z+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi])\subset U_{1,-},
or

(5.47)

(z+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi])\cap(U_{1,-}\cap\ell_{2,2})\neq\emptyset,\quad(z+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi])\cap(U_{1,+}\cap\ell_{2,2})=\emptyset.

Then,
\Lambda^{\prime}_{-}
is a finite set.
In particular, we have

(5.48)

(z+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi])\cap U_{1,-}\neq\emptyset,\ \forall z\in\Lambda^{\prime}_{-};\quad(z+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi])\cap U_{1,+}\neq\emptyset,\ \forall z\in(\mathbb{H}\cap\Lambda^{\prime})\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}_{-}.

Denote by
W_{-}
the union of the set
\{z\in\mathbb{H}\cup\mathbb{R}:|z-z_{c}|\leq n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}\}\cap U_{1,-}
and the
n^{-3}
neighborhood of
[w_{-},z_{c}-n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}]\setminus P_{1,-}
in
U
.
Denote by
W_{+}
the
n^{-3}
neighborhood of
(-\infty,w_{-}]\setminus P_{1,-}
in
U
. See Figure
7
for an illustration of these sets.
By Lemma
5.18
, we have
W_{-}\subset U_{1,-}
and
U_{1,-}\cap W_{+}=\emptyset
.
Thus, we have

\{z\in\Lambda^{\prime}:z+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi]\subset W_{-}\}\subset\Lambda^{\prime}_{-},\quad\{z\in\Lambda^{\prime}:z+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi]\subset W_{+}\}\cap\Lambda^{\prime}_{-}=\emptyset.

Now, consider the set
\cup_{z\in\Lambda^{\prime}_{-}}(z+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi])
, and denote its connected component that intersects
W_{-}
as
W_{*}
.
The boundary of
W_{*}
is a
\Lambda
-curve, which contains a vertex in
\Lambda
(denoted by
z_{*,c}
) within distance
5\xi
to
z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\vartheta_{1}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}
.
It also contains
[w_{-},z_{c}-5\xi]\setminus P_{1,-}
and is disjoint from
(-\infty,w_{-}]\setminus P_{1,-}
.
We denote its left-most intersection with
P_{1,-}
as
z_{*,l}
.
We let
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
be the part of the boundary of
W_{*}
, going from
z_{*,c}
to
z_{*,l}
counter-clockwisely. See Figure
7
for an illustration of
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
and these related objects.

z_{*,l}
z_{*,c}
U_{1,+}
U_{1,-}
w_{-}
z_{c}
z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\vartheta_{1}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}
z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\theta_{2}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}
\ell_{2,2}
\ell_{1,1}
P_{1,-}
W_{*}
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
W_{-}
W_{+}

Figure 7
.
An illustration of discretizing
\ell_{1,1}
into
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
.

Next, we check that
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
constructed this way satisfies the requirements (1), (2), and (3).

(1)

By (
5.48
),
\Lambda^{\prime}_{-}
is contained in the
\sqrt{2}\xi
neighborhood of
U_{1,-}
, and
(\mathbb{H}\cap\Lambda^{\prime})\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}_{-}
is contained in the
\sqrt{2}\xi
neighborhood of
U_{1,+}
. Hence, any point in
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
is within distance
2\sqrt{2}\xi
to both
U_{1,-}
and
U_{1,+}
, thereby within distance
2\sqrt{2}\xi
to
\ell_{1,1}\cup[z_{c}\to z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\vartheta_{1}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}]
. As
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
is from
z_{*,c}
to
z_{*,l}
, we have that it must be contained in the
5\xi
neighborhood of
\ell_{1,1}
.

(2)

This follows from (1) and Lemma
5.18
.

(3)

Take any
z_{0}\in\ell^{*}_{1,1}\cap\ell^{*}_{2,2}
, we can find
z_{0,-},z_{0,+}\in\Lambda^{\prime}\cap\ell^{*}_{2,2}
such that
|z_{0,+}-z_{0,-}|=2\xi
,
z_{0}=(z_{0,+}+z_{0,-})/2
, and
z_{0,-}
,
z_{0,+}
are in different sides of
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
. Then, only one of them is in
W_{*}
, and hence only one of them is in
\Lambda^{\prime}_{-}
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that

z_{0,-}\in\Lambda^{\prime}_{-}
and
z_{0,+}\in(\mathbb{H}\cap\Lambda^{\prime})\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}_{-}
.

As
z_{0,+}\in\ell^{*}_{2,2}
, we can find
z_{b,+}\in\ell_{2,2}\cap(z_{0,+}+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi])
. We can always choose
z_{b,+}
so that
z_{b,+}\in U_{1,+}
, since otherwise
z_{0,+}\in\Lambda^{\prime}_{-}
by (
5.47
).
On the other hand, we can also find
z_{b,-}\in\ell_{2,2}\cap(z_{0,-}+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi])
.
Since
z_{0,-}\in\Lambda^{\prime}_{-}
, if
(z_{0,-}+[-\xi,\xi]+\mathbf{i}[-\xi,\xi])\subset U_{1,-}
, we can always choose
z_{b,-}
such that
z_{b,-}\in U_{1,-}
.
Now, we have found
z_{b,-}\in U_{1,-}\cap\ell_{2,2}
and
z_{b,+}\in U_{1,+}\cap\ell_{2,2}
, with
|z_{b,-}-z_{0}|,|z_{b,+}-z_{0}|<3\xi
.
Then, we have
z_{b,-},z_{b,+}\in U
(since
\ell_{2,2}\subset U
), and

(5.49)

\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{b,-}))=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{b,+}))=0.

Also, notice that

(5.50)

\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,-}))>0,

since
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\mathsf{D}_{1}>0
on
U_{1,-}\setminus\mathbb{R}
and
\ell_{2,2}\cap\mathbb{R}=\emptyset
.

We next claim that

(5.51)

\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,+}))\leq 0.

Otherwise,
z_{b,-}
and
z_{b,+}
must be in different components of
U\setminus\ell_{1,2}
, so
[z_{b,-}\to z_{b,+}]
(which is in
U
and has length
\lesssim\xi
) intersects both
\ell_{1,1}
and
\ell_{1,2}
.
Take any
z_{b,1}\in[z_{b,-}\to z_{b,+}]\cap\ell_{1,1}
and
z_{b,2}\in[z_{b,-}\to z_{b,+}]\cap\ell_{1,2}
.
By property (2) above, we have that
z_{b,1}
and
z_{b,2}
are not in the
n^{-3}/3
neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}\setminus P_{1,-}
, so Lemma
5.12
implies that
\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,1})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,2})\lesssim n^{2}\xi
.
On the other hand, since
z_{b,1}\in\ell_{1,1}
and
z_{b,2}\in\ell_{1,2}
, we have
\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,1})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c}),\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,2})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c})\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,1})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c})\geq\mathsf{D}_{1}(e^{\mathbf{i}\vartheta_{1}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c})\asymp n^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}}
,
-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,2})+\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c})\geq-\mathsf{D}_{1}(e^{\mathbf{i}\vartheta_{2}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c})+\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c})\asymp n^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}}
by (the
\mathsf{D}_{1}
version of) (
5.31
).
Hence, we have
\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,1})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,2})\gtrsim n^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}}
, which leads to a contradiction. Thus, we conclude (
5.51
).

Now, combining (
5.49
), (
5.50
), and (
5.51
), we obtain that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,-})-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{b,-}))>0\geq\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,+})-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{b,+}))
.
Thus, there exists some
z_{b,*}\in[z_{b,-}\to z_{b,+}]
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{b,*})-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{b,*}))=0
, i.e.,
z_{b,*}\in\ell_{d}
. Note that we must have
|z_{b,*}-z_{0}|<3\xi
since
|z_{b,-}-z_{0}|,|z_{b,+}-z_{0}|<3\xi
, so
z_{0}
is contained in the
3\xi
-neighborhood of
\ell_{d}
.

Finally, the construction of
\ell^{*}_{1,3}
and corresponding properties follow similar arguments.
∎

We are now ready to finish the proof of Lemma
5.19
by deforming the contours to
\ell^{*}_{2,2}
and
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
,
\ell^{*}_{1,3}
.

Proof of Lemma
5.19
: Outer part.

As stated above, we just need to control (
5.45
) over the contours stated in Lemma
5.19
(Outer part).
For the convenience of notation, we introduce the following definitions.
Let
L_{1}
be the contour of
[z_{c}+e^{3\pi\mathbf{i}/4}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}\to\iota(\ell^{*}_{1,1})]
followed by
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
, where
\iota(\ell^{*}_{1,1})
is the starting point of
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
, with
|\iota(\ell^{*}_{1,1})-(z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\vartheta_{1}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c})|<5\xi
.
Let
L_{2}
be the contour of
[z_{c}+e^{\pi\mathbf{i}/2}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}\to\iota(\ell^{*}_{2,2})]
followed by
\ell^{*}_{2,2}
, where
\iota(\ell^{*}_{2,2})
is the starting point of
\ell^{*}_{2,2}
, with
|\iota(\ell^{*}_{2,2})-(z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\theta_{2}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c})|<2\xi
.

We first claim that there exists a constant
c>0
such that

(5.52)

\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(w)-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c}))>cn^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}},\quad\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z)-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{c}))<-cn^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}},

for any
w\in L_{1}
and
z\in L_{2}
.

We now prove this claim. For any
w\in\ell^{*}_{1,1}
, by Lemma
5.25
we can find
w^{\prime}\in\ell_{1,1}
such that
|w-w^{\prime}|<5\xi
; and
w
is disjoint from the
n^{-3}/2
-neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}\setminus P_{1,-}
. By Lemma
5.17
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(w^{\prime})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c}))>cn^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}}
. Then, by Lemma
5.12
, the first inequality in (
5.52
) holds for any
w\in\ell^{*}_{1,1}
.
For any
w\in[z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\theta_{1}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}\to\iota(\ell^{*}_{1,1})]
, we must have that
|w-(z_{c}+e^{\mathbf{i}\vartheta_{1}}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c})|\lesssim n^{-1/4-\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}
by Lemma
5.17
and Lemma
5.25
. Then, using Lemma
5.14
, we obtain the first inequality in (
5.52
).
The second inequality in (
5.52
) can be proved in a similar way by using Lemmas
5.12
,
5.14
,
5.17
, and
5.23
.

We next analyze the contours in the outer part of Lemma
5.19
, which can be further deformed and decomposed into several parts. By symmetry, it suffices to consider the following few cases.

(a)

The contour of
z
is
L_{2}^{\prime}:=[z_{c}+e^{3\pi\mathbf{i}/2}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}\to z_{c}+e^{\pi\mathbf{i}/2}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}]
, and the contour of
w
is
L_{1}
.
We note that
L_{1}
is deformed from
[z_{c}+e^{3\pi\mathbf{i}/4}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}\to-t_{c}-1]
, and this deformation is allowed because the integrand in (
5.45
) has no
w
pole in
\mathbb{H}
or
[-t_{c}-1,w_{-}]\cup P_{1,-}
.
Note that the contours
L_{2}^{\prime}
and
L_{1}
do not intersect, and the distance between them is of order
\gtrsim n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}
by Lemma
5.25
and the fact that the distance between
\ell_{1,1}
and
L_{2}^{\prime}
is of order
\gtrsim n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}
.

For any
z\in L_{2}^{\prime}
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z)-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{c}))\leq Cn^{-1+2\epsilon_{2}}
for a constant
C>0
by Lemma
5.14
(note it can be negative).
Then, by (
5.52
), for any
z\in L_{2}^{\prime}
and
w\in L_{1}
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z)-\mathsf{D}_{1}(w)-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{c})+\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c}))<-cn^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}}
.
By Lemma
5.13
,
\ell_{1,1}
is contained in a ball of radius
\lesssim n
. Thus,
L_{1}
is also contained in a ball of radius
\lesssim n
, from which we see that its length satisfies
\lesssim n^{2}\xi^{-1}
.
Now, the integral (
5.45
) along these contours is at most of order

n^{2}\xi^{-1}\cdot n^{1/4-\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}^{-1}\cdot\exp(-cn^{4\epsilon_{2}})\lesssim\exp(-cn^{4\epsilon_{2}}/2).

(b)

The contour of
w
is
L_{1}^{\prime}:=[z_{c}+e^{5\pi\mathbf{i}/4}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}\to z_{c}\to z_{c}+e^{3\pi\mathbf{i}/4}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}]
, and the contour of
z
is
L_{2}
.
We note that
L_{2}
is deformed from
[z_{c}+e^{\pi\mathbf{i}/2}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}\to\infty e^{\pi\mathbf{i}/2}]
. This deformation is allowed because the integrand of (
5.45
) has no
z
pole in
\mathbb{H}
, and
|\exp(n\mathsf{D}_{2}(z))|

decays exponentially along any direction between
\mathbf{i}
and the asymptotic direction of
\ell^{*}_{2,2}
, due to Lemma
5.13
. Not that the contours
L_{1}^{\prime}
and
L_{2}
do not intersect, and the distance between them is of order
\gtrsim n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}
by Lemma
5.23
and the fact that the distance between
\ell_{2,2}
and
L_{1}^{\prime}
is of order
\gtrsim n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}
.

For any
w\in L_{1}^{\prime}
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(-\mathsf{D}_{1}(w)+\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c}))\leq Cn^{-1+2\epsilon_{2}}
by Lemma
5.14
.
Then, by (
5.52
), for any
z\in L_{2}
and
w\in L_{1}^{\prime}
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z)-\mathsf{D}_{1}(w)-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{c})+\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c}))<-cn^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}}
.
In addition, if
|z|>n
, by Lemma
5.13
there is

(5.53)

\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z)-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{c}))<-c|z|.

Therefore, the integral (
5.45
) along these contours is at most of order

n^{2}\xi^{-1}\cdot n^{1/4-\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}^{-1}\cdot\exp(-cn^{4\epsilon_{2}})+\sum_{W\in\mathbb{Z},W>n}W\xi^{-1}\cdot n^{1/4-\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}^{-1}\cdot\exp(-cnW)\lesssim\exp(-cn^{4\epsilon_{2}}/2),

where the first term accounts for the part of
L_{2}
where
|z|\leq n
(and hence has length
\lesssim n^{2}\xi^{-1}
), and the summand for each
W
accounts for the part of
L_{2}
where
||z|-W|\leq 1
(and hence has length
\lesssim W\xi^{-1}
).

(c)

The contour of
w
is
L_{1}
, and the contour of
z
is the complex conjugate of
L_{2}
. The distance between them is
\gtrsim n^{-3}
by Lemmas
5.23
and
5.25
.
By bounding the lengths of the contours as in (a) and (b), and using (
5.52
) and (
5.53
), we conclude that the integral (
5.45
) along these contours is also
\lesssim\exp(-cn^{4\epsilon_{2}})
.

(d)

The contour of
w
is
L_{1}
, and the contour of
z
is
L_{2}
.
These contours
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
may intersect, since
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
and
\ell^{*}_{2,2}
may intersect. (As we have seen in (a) and (b), the distance between
[z_{c}+e^{3\pi\mathbf{i}/4}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}\to\iota(\ell^{*}_{1,1})]
and
\ell^{*}_{2,2}
is of order
\gtrsim n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}
, and the distance between
[z_{c}+e^{3\pi\mathbf{i}/4}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}\to\iota(\ell^{*}_{2,2})]
and
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
is also of order
\gtrsim n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}
.)
Denote
S=\ell^{*}_{1,1}\cap\ell^{*}_{2,2}=L_{1}\cap L_{2}
.
Since
\ell^{*}_{1,1}
is a
\Lambda
-curve and
\ell^{*}_{2,2}
is a
\Lambda^{\prime}
-curve,
S
is contained in the lattice
\Lambda+\{\xi,\mathbf{i}\xi\}=\Lambda^{\prime}+\{\xi,\mathbf{i}\xi\}
. Using the fact that
L_{1}
is contained in a ball of radius
\lesssim n
as shown above, we get the trivial bound
|S|\lesssim(n\xi^{-1})^{2}
.
For any
w\in L_{1}
and
z\in L_{2}
, unless
w,z\in z_{*}+(-\xi,\xi)+\mathbf{i}(-\xi,\xi)
for some
z_{*}\in S
, we must have
|w-z|\geq\xi
.
For each
z_{*}\in S
, the parts of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
inside
z_{*}+(-\xi,\xi)+\mathbf{i}(-\xi,\xi)
are two orthogonal line segments, each having length
2\xi
.
Therefore, by (
5.52
), the integral (
5.45
) over these segments is
\lesssim\xi^{-1}\exp(-cn^{4\epsilon_{2}})
.
Summing over all
z_{*}\in S
and bounding the integral (
5.45
) over the rest parts of the
w
and
z
contours as in (a) and (b), we again get the bound
\lesssim\exp(-cn^{4\epsilon_{2}})
.

In case (d), the
w
and
z
contours are deformed from
[z_{c}+e^{3\pi\mathbf{i}/4}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}\to-t_{c}-1]
and
[z_{c}+e^{\pi\mathbf{i}/2}n^{-1/4+\epsilon_{2}}t_{c}\to\infty e^{\pi\mathbf{i}/2}]
, respectively.
When
S\neq\emptyset
, this procedure potentially leads to residues at
w=z
, which are given by

(5.54)

\int dz\exp(n\mathsf{D}_{2}(z)-n\mathsf{D}_{1}(z)-n\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{c})+n\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c})),

where the integral is along some curves
[z_{*,1}\to z_{*,2}]
,
\ldots
,
[z_{*,2k-1}\to z_{*,2k}]
, such that
\{z_{*,i}\}_{i=1}^{2k}=S
.

We assume that
S\neq\emptyset
and bound the integral (
5.54
) along
[z_{*,2i-1}\to z_{*,2i}]
for each
i=1,\ldots,k
.
By Lemmas
5.24
and
5.25
, the curve
\ell_{d}\neq\emptyset
and we can find
z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime},z_{*,2i}^{\prime\prime}\in\ell_{d}
and a
\Lambda^{\prime}
-curve
L_{*,i}
with endpoints
z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime}
,
z_{*,2i}^{\prime}
, such that
|z_{*,2i-1}-z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime}|,|z_{*,2i}-z_{*,2i}^{\prime\prime}|\leq 5\xi
, and
|z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime}-z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime},|z_{*,2i}^{\prime}-z_{*,2i}^{\prime\prime}|\leq 2\xi
.
In addition, the
\Lambda^{\prime}
-curve
L_{*,i}
is contained in the
2\xi
neighborhood of
\ell_{d}
between
z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime}
and
z_{*,2i}^{\prime\prime}
, and is disjoint from the
n^{-3}/2
-neighborhood of
E(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2})
.

We deform the contour
[z_{*,2i-1}\to z_{*,2i}]
into the contour consisting of
[z_{*,2i-1}\to z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime}]
,
L_{*,i}
, and
[z_{*,2i}^{\prime}\to z_{*,2i}]
.
For each
z
on this contour,
by (
5.46
) and the fact that
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2})
is monotone along
\ell_{d}
, we have that

(5.55)

\begin{split}&\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z)-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z))<\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z^{\prime\prime})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z^{\prime\prime}))+\OO(\xi n^{2})\\
<&\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime}))\vee\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{*,2i}^{\prime\prime})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{*,2i}^{\prime\prime}))+\OO(\xi n^{2})\\
<&\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{*,2i-1})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{*,2i-1}))\vee\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{*,2i})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{*,2i}))+\OO(\xi n^{2}),\end{split}

where
z^{\prime\prime}
is a point in
\ell_{d}
between
z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime}
and
z_{*,2i}^{\prime\prime}
, with
|z^{\prime\prime}-z|\leq 5\xi
.
Using Lemma
5.12
and the facts that
z_{*,2i-1}\in S\subset\ell^{*}_{1,1}
and
\ell_{1,1}
is disjoint from the
n^{-3}
neighborhood of
E(\mathsf{D}_{1})
by Lemma
5.18
, we obtain that

(5.56)

\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{*,2i-1}))>\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime\prime}))+\OO(\xi n^{2}),

for some
z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\ell_{1,1}
with
|z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime\prime}-z_{*,2i-1}|\lesssim\xi
. By Lemma
5.17
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime\prime})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c}))>cn^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}}
, which, together with (
5.56
), implies that
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{*,2i-1})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c}))>cn^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}}
.
Similarly, we have

\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{*,2i})-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c}))>cn^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}},\quad\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{*,2i-1})-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{c})),\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{*,2i})-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{c}))<-cn^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}}.

Combining the above estimates with (
5.55
), we conclude that

(5.57)

\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathsf{D}_{2}(z)-\mathsf{D}_{1}(z)-\mathsf{D}_{2}(z_{c})+\mathsf{D}_{1}(z_{c}))<-cn^{-1+4\epsilon_{2}}.

We next bound the length of
L_{*,i}
.
Since
z_{*,2i-1},z_{*,2i}\in S\subset\ell^{*}_{1,1}
, we have that
z_{*,2i-1},z_{*,2i}\lesssim n
by Lemma
5.25
, because
\ell_{1,1}
is contained in a ball of radius
\lesssim n
as discussed above.
We claim that
L_{*,i}
is also contained in a ball of radius
\lesssim n
.
First, we have
z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime},z_{*,2i}^{\prime\prime}\lesssim n
.
Next, consider the semi-circle
\{z\in\mathbb{H}:|z|=r\}
for
r=|z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime}|+|z_{*,2i}^{\prime\prime}|+n
.
Since
\ell_{d}\neq\emptyset
, by Lemma
5.22
we have either (i)
{\mathsf{x}}_{1}-{\mathsf{t}}_{1}<{\mathsf{x}}_{2}-{\mathsf{t}}_{2}+1
and
{\mathsf{x}}_{1}>{\mathsf{x}}_{2}-1
, or (ii)
{\mathsf{x}}_{1}-{\mathsf{t}}_{1}>{\mathsf{x}}_{2}-{\mathsf{t}}_{2}+1
and
{\mathsf{x}}_{1}<{\mathsf{x}}_{2}-1
.
In either case, the function
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathsf{D}_{1}-\mathsf{D}_{2})
is strictly monotone along the semi-circle.
Therefore, the semi-circle
\{z\in\mathbb{H}:|z|=r\}
intersects
\ell_{d}
(which is contained in
\mathbb{H}
and goes to
\infty
) exactly once, so the intersection cannot lie on the part of
\ell_{d}
between
z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime}
and
z_{*,2i}^{\prime\prime}
.
As a result, the part of
\ell_{d}
between
z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime\prime}
and
z_{*,2i}^{\prime\prime}
is contained in a ball of radius
\lesssim n
, and the claim holds.
Thus, the length of
L_{*,i}
is at most
\lesssim n^{2}\xi^{-1}
.

Using the estimate (
5.57
), the bound on the length of
L_{*,i}
, and
|z_{*,2i-1}-z^{\prime}_{*,2i-1}|,|z_{*,2i}-z_{*,2i}^{\prime}|\lesssim\xi
, we conclude that the integral (
5.54
) along
[z_{*,2i-1}\to z_{*,2i-1}^{\prime}]
,
L_{*,i}
, and
[z_{*,2i}^{\prime}\to z_{*,2i}]
is at most
\lesssim\exp(-cn^{4\epsilon_{2}})
.
Since
|S|\lesssim(n\xi^{-1})^{2}
(by Lemma
5.13
), summing over
i
yields the desired bound
\lesssim\exp(-cn^{4\epsilon_{2}})
.
∎

6.
Optimal rigidity around cusps

In this section, we prove the optimal height function concentration estimate for random lozenge tilings around cusps, which will imply the first part of
4.2
.

6.1.
Concentration of height function

Fix a rational polygonal set
{\mathfrak{P}}
satisfying
2.5
, and denote its liquid region and arctic curve by
\mathfrak{L}=\mathfrak{L}(\mathfrak{P})
and
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}(\mathfrak{P})
, respectively.
Let
H^{*}
denote the limiting height function of
{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Let
n
be a large integer such that
\mathsf{P}=n{\mathfrak{P}}
is a tileable domain, and let
\mathsf{H}
denote the height function associated with the uniformly random tiling of
\mathsf{P}
, that has boundary value
nH^{*}
on
\partial{\mathsf{P}}
.
As in
Section

4
, all the constants in this section (including those hidden in
\lesssim,\gtrsim,\asymp,\OO
) can depend on
{\mathfrak{P}}
.

We recall the following height function concentration statement from
[
Hua21
]
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Take any constant
\delta>0
.
Let
\mathfrak{L}_{+}(\mathfrak{P})=\{u\in\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}}:\dist(u,\mathfrak{L})\leq n^{\delta-2/3}\}
be the augmented liquid region.
Then, the following two statements hold with overwhelming probability.

(1)

\big|\mathsf{H}(nv)-nH^{*}(v)\big|<n^{\delta}
for any
v\in\overline{\mathfrak{P}}
.

(2)

For any
v\in\overline{\mathfrak{P}}\setminus\mathfrak{L}_{+}(\mathfrak{P})
, we have
\mathsf{H}(nv)=nH^{*}(v)
.

Theorem
6.1
does not give optimal rigidity estimates close to cusp locations. In this section, we prove an optimal version, as stated in the following theorem.

Theorem 6.2
.

Fix a cusp point
(x_{c},t_{c})\in\mathfrak{A}
. By possibly rotating
{\mathfrak{P}}
by
180^{\circ}
, the arctic curve
{\mathfrak{A}}
in a neighborhood of
(x_{c},t_{c})
consists of two analytic pieces
\{(E_{-}(t),t):t_{c}-{\mathfrak{s}}\leq t\leq t_{c}\}
and
\{(E_{+}(t),t):t_{c}-{\mathfrak{s}}\leq t\leq t_{c}\}
, for some small constant
{\mathfrak{s}}>0
.
Then, for any constant
\delta>0
, the following holds with overwhelming probability: for any
v=(x,t)
such that
t_{c}-{\mathfrak{s}}\leq t\leq t_{c}
and
x\in[E_{-}(t)+(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}n^{\delta-2/3},E_{+}(t)-(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}n^{\delta-2/3}]
, there is

(6.1)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{H}}(nv)=nH^{*}(v),

Remark 6.3
.

When
t_{c}-t\asymp 1
, the statement (
6.1
) reduces to item (2) in
6.1
. However, when
t_{c}-t\ll 1
, (
6.1
) is stronger than
6.1
. Such optimal height function concentration is crucial for the tiling path estimate (
4.2
), where we consider a mesoscopic box around the cusp location
(x_{c},t_{c})
.

This optimal rigidity estimate will be proved based on an estimate from
[
Hua21
]
on random lozenge tilings in a trapezoid with random boundary, stated as
6.4
below, and a comparison argument invoking
3.3
. For this, we start by carving out a trapezoid domain from
{\mathfrak{P}}
around a cusp.

6.2.
Trapezoid domain

As
{\mathfrak{P}}
satisfies
2.5
,
for definiteness, for the rest of this section, we assume (without loss of generality) that the axis
\ell
in
2.5
is the horizontal axis
\{{\mathsf{t}}=0\}
.
We take a cusp point
(x_{c},t_{c})\in\mathfrak{A}
. Then,
(x_{c},t_{c})
is not a tangency location.
By possibly rotating
{\mathfrak{P}}
by
180^{\circ}
, we can assume that the cusp ‘points upwards’, i.e., in a small neighborhood of
(x_{c},t_{c})
the arctic curve
{\mathfrak{A}}
is contained on or below the line
t=t_{c}
.
Note this notion is weaker than ‘upward oriented’ from
Definition

2.6
, because the axis
\ell
is now fixed as the horizontal axis.
Next, we carve out a
trapezoid
around
(x_{c},t_{c})
.

{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}
{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}
\partial_{\north}(\mathfrak{D})
\partial_{\ea}(\mathfrak{D})
\partial_{\we}(\mathfrak{D})
\partial_{\so}(\mathfrak{D})

Figure 8
.
Shown above are the four possibilities for
\mathfrak{D}
.

A
trapezoid
is a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the following form

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}=\big\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times[{\mathfrak{t}}_{0},{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}]:\mathfrak{a}(t)\leq x\leq\mathfrak{b}(t)\big\},

where
{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}<{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}
, and
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}
are linear functions on
[{\mathfrak{t}}_{0},{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}]
with
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}(t),\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}(t)\in\{0,1\}
and
\mathfrak{a}(t)\leq\mathfrak{b}(t)
for each
t\in[{\mathfrak{t}}_{0},{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}]
. We denote its four boundaries by

(6.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{\so}(\mathfrak{D})=\big\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}:t={\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\big\};\qquad\quad\partial_{\north}(\mathfrak{D})=\big\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}:t={\mathfrak{t}}_{1}\big\};\\
&\partial_{\we}(\mathfrak{D})=\big\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}:x=\mathfrak{a}(t)\big\};\qquad\partial_{\ea}(\mathfrak{D})=\big\{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}:x=\mathfrak{b}(t)\big\}.\end{aligned}

We refer to
Figure

8
for a depiction.

We now construct the trapezoid
\mathfrak{D}
associated with
(x_{c},t_{c})
.
Let
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
be the maximal and minimal numbers such that
x_{1}<x_{0}<x_{2}
,
u_{1}=(x_{1},t_{c})\in\mathfrak{A}
, and
u_{2}=(x_{2},t_{c})\in\mathfrak{A}
. By
2.5
, neither
u_{1}
nor
u_{2}
is a cusp of
\mathfrak{A}
. If
u_{1}\in\partial\mathfrak{P}
, then it is a (non-horizontal) tangency location of
\mathfrak{A}
, so it lies along a side of
\partial\mathfrak{P}
with slope
1
or
\infty
. We then let this side contain the west boundary of
\mathfrak{D}
. If instead
u_{1}\notin\partial\mathfrak{P}
, then there exist
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\mathfrak{P},u)>0
and
r=r(\mathfrak{P},u)\in(0,\varepsilon)
such that the radius
r
disk
\mathbb{B}_{r}(x_{1}-\varepsilon,t_{c})
does not intersect
\overline{\mathfrak{L}}
. Then, depending on whether
\nabla H^{*}(x_{1},t_{c})=(1,-1)
or
\nabla H^{*}(x_{1},t_{c})\in\big\{(0,0),(1,0)\big\}
(one of them must hold by the first statement of
2.4
), the west boundary of
\mathfrak{D}
is contained in the segment obtained as the intersection between
\mathbb{B}_{r}(x_{1}-\varepsilon,t_{c})
and the line passing through
(x_{1}-\varepsilon,t_{c})
with slope
1
or
\infty
, respectively.

So far, we’ve specified a segment containing the west boundary of
\mathfrak{D}
, and one containing its east boundary can be specified similarly. Then, we choose the interval
[{\mathfrak{t}}_{0},{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}]
as
{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}=t_{c}-{\mathfrak{s}}
and
{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}=t_{c}+{\mathfrak{s}}
, where
{\mathfrak{s}}
is chosen sufficiently small so that the east and west boundary of
\mathfrak{D}
are contained in the segments specified above. These information determine the trapezoid
{\mathfrak{D}}
associated with
(x_{c},t_{c})
.

{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}
{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}
{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}
{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}
I_{t}^{+}
(E^{\prime}_{-}(t_{1}),t_{1})
(E^{\prime}_{+}(t_{2}),t_{2})
(x_{c},t_{c})
E^{\prime}_{-}(t)
E_{-}(t)
E_{+}(t)
E^{\prime}_{+}(t)
\mathfrak{L}({\mathfrak{D}})
\mathfrak{L}({\mathfrak{D}})

Figure 9
.
Left: the tangency locations
(E^{\prime}_{-}(t_{1}),t_{1}),(E^{\prime}_{+}(t_{2}),t_{2})
, and the cusp
(x_{c},t_{c})
. Right: the enlarged liquid region, with its time slices
I_{t}^{+}
as in (
6.5
) and (
6.6
).

In summary, for a polygonal set
{\mathfrak{P}}
satisfying
2.5
, and a cusp point
(x_{c},t_{c})\in\mathfrak{A}
, (by possibly rotating
{\mathfrak{P}}
by
180^{\circ}
) we can carve out a trapezoid
{\mathfrak{D}}
contained in
{\mathfrak{P}}
and the time strip
[{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}=t_{c}-{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}=t_{c}+{\mathfrak{s}}]
for a small enough
{\mathfrak{s}}>0
, such that the followings hold.

(1)

The limiting height function
H^{*}
is constant along both
\partial_{\ea}(\mathfrak{D})
and
\partial_{\we}(\mathfrak{D})
.

(2)

Denote by
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}})={\mathfrak{D}}\cap{\mathfrak{A}}
the arctic curve in
{\mathfrak{D}}
,
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}})={\mathfrak{D}}\cap{\mathfrak{L}}
the liquid region restricted to
{\mathfrak{D}}
, and by
I^{*}_{t}
the closure of
\{x:(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{D}})\}
for each
{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\leq t\leq{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}
.
Then,
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}})=\{(E_{-}(t),t),(E_{+}(t),t):{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\leq t\leq t_{c}\}\cup\{(E^{\prime}_{-}(t),t),(E^{\prime}_{+}(t),t):{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\leq t\leq{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}\}
(See Figure
9
). For
t_{c}\leq t\leq{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}
, the slice
I^{*}_{t}
is a single interval

I^{*}_{t}=\big[E_{-}^{\prime}(t),E_{+}^{\prime}(t)\big];

for
{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\leq t\leq t_{c}
, the slice
I^{*}_{t}
consists of two intervals

I^{*}_{t}=\big[E^{\prime}_{-}(t),E_{-}(t)\big]\cup\big[E_{+}(t),E^{\prime}_{+}(t)\big]
.
The complement
\{x:(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{D}}\}\setminus I^{*}_{t}
consists of several intervals. On each interval, we have either
\partial_{x}H^{*}(x,t)\equiv 0

or
\partial_{x}H^{*}(x,t)\equiv 1
.

(3)

Any tangency location along
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}})
is of the form
\min I_{t}^{*}
or
\max I_{t}^{*}
for some
t\in({\mathfrak{t}}_{0},{\mathfrak{t}}_{1})
. At most one tangency location is of the form
\min I_{t}^{*}
, and at most one is of the form
\max I_{t}^{*}
. Moreover, these tangent locations are contained in either
\partial_{\we}({\mathfrak{D}})
or
\partial_{\ea}({\mathfrak{D}})
.

6.3.
Lozenge tiling in a trapezoid with random boundary

For the trapezoid
{\mathfrak{D}}
given above, we next state an optimal rigidity estimate for uniformly random lozenge tilings on it, with a
random
north boundary height function.
To state it, we need an enlarged version of the time slice

I^{*}_{t}
.
Fix an arbitrarily small constant
{\mathfrak{d}}>0
.
For any
\big(E^{\prime}_{\pm}(t),t\big)
on the arctic curve
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}})
, we define the distance function

(6.4)

\displaystyle\tau\big(E^{\prime}_{\pm}(t),t\big)=|t-t_{c}|^{2/3}n^{6{\mathfrak{d}}-2/3}\vee n^{-1+10{\mathfrak{d}}}.

Moreover, for any
\big(E_{\pm}(t),t\big)
on the arctic curve
{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{D}})
with
t\leq t_{c}
, we define the distance function
\tau\big(E_{\pm}(t),t\big):=(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}n^{6{\mathfrak{d}}-2/3}
.
We then define the enlarged intervals: for
t_{c}\leq t\leq{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}
,

(6.5)

\displaystyle I_{t}^{+}=\big[E^{\prime}_{-}(t)-\tau(E^{\prime}_{-}(t),t),E^{\prime}_{+}(t)+\tau(E^{\prime}_{+}(t),t)\big],

and for
{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\leq t<t_{c}
,

(6.6)

\displaystyle I_{t}^{+}=\big[E^{\prime}_{-}(t)-\tau(E^{\prime}_{-}(t),t),E_{-}(t)+\tau(E_{-}(t),t)\big]\cup\big[E_{+}(t)-\tau(E_{+}(t),t),E^{\prime}_{+}(t)+\tau(E^{\prime}_{+}(t),t)\big].

By
7.1
below,
E_{+}(t)-E_{-}(t)\asymp(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}
. Hence, for
t\geq t_{c}-n^{-1/2+4{\mathfrak{d}}}
, (
6.6
) reduces to a single interval

(6.7)

\displaystyle I_{t}^{+}=\left[E^{\prime}_{-}(t)-\tau\big(E^{\prime}_{-}(t),t\big),E^{\prime}_{+}(t)+\tau\big(E^{\prime}_{+}(t),t\big)\right].

See Figure
9
for an illustration.

Due to some rounding issues, the set
n{\mathfrak{D}}
may not be a tileable domain for lozenge tilings. Therefore, we use the notions of plausible boundary height functions and uniformly random height functions from
Definition

3.2
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Denote
\mathsf{D}=n\mathfrak{D}
.
Let
{\mathsf{h}}:\partial{\mathsf{D}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a plausible boundary height functions of
\mathsf{D}
, such that (1)
{\mathsf{h}}
is constant on
\partial_{\rm we}{\mathsf{D}}
and on
\partial_{\rm ea}{\mathsf{D}}
, respectively, and (2) on
\partial_{\rm so}{\mathsf{D}}
,

(6.8)

\begin{split}\big|{\mathsf{h}}(nv)-nH^{*}(v)\big|\leq n^{\mathfrak{d}/3},\quad&\text{for}\quad v=(x,{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}),\quad\dist(x,I_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}}^{*})\leq n^{{\mathfrak{d}}/3-2/3},\\
{\mathsf{h}}(nv)=nH^{*}(v),\quad&\text{for}\quad v=(x,{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}),\quad\dist(x,I_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}}^{*})>n^{{\mathfrak{d}}/3-2/3}.\end{split}

Then, there exists a
random plausible boundary height function

\widetilde{\mathsf{h}}
of
{\mathsf{D}}
, which is equal to
{\mathsf{h}}
on
\partial_{\rm we}{\mathsf{D}}\cup\partial_{\rm ea}{\mathsf{D}}\cup\partial_{\rm so}{\mathsf{D}}
, such that the following holds with overwhelming probability. Denote by
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
the uniformly random height function of
\mathsf{D}
with boundary
\widetilde{\mathsf{h}}
. For any
{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\leq t\leq{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}
, we have

(6.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big|\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}(nv)-nH^{*}(v)\big|\leq n^{3{\mathfrak{d}}},\quad&\text{for}\quad v=(x,t),\quad x\in I_{t}^{+},\\
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}(nv)=nH^{*}(v),\quad&\text{for}\quad v=(x,t),\quad x\not\in I_{t}^{+}.\end{aligned}

This proposition is the same as
[
Hua21
, Proposition 4.4]
, via the equivalence between lozenge tilings and non-intersecting Bernoulli paths stated in Section
2.1.2
.

With the above preparations, we complete the proof of
6.2
using the height function comparison (i.e.,
3.3
) between the uniformly random tiling of
n{\mathfrak{P}}
and the uniformly random tiling of
n{\mathfrak{D}}
(carved out around a cusp point) with random boundary.

Proof of
6.2
.

We carve out a trapezoid
{\mathfrak{D}}
around the cusp point
(x_{c},t_{c})
, as given above, and denote
{\mathsf{D}}=n{\mathfrak{D}}
.
By
2.4
,
\nabla H^{*}\equiv(0,0)
,
(1,0)
, or
(1,-1)
in
\{(x,t):{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\leq t\leq t_{c},x\in[E_{-}(t),E_{+}(t)]\}
.
For the rest of the proof, we assume the first case, while the proofs in the other two cases are very similar and thus omitted.
This assumption implies that
H^{*}
is constant in this region. We assume that
H^{*}\equiv 0
without loss of generality, as we can always add a global constant to
H^{*}
.

Take an arbitrarily small constant
{\mathfrak{d}}>0
.
We denote by
\Omega
the set of plausible boundary height functions
{\mathsf{h}}:\partial{\mathsf{D}}\to\mathbb{R}
, such that (1)
{\mathsf{h}}
is constant on
\partial_{\rm we}{\mathsf{D}}
and on
\partial_{\rm ea}{\mathsf{D}}
, respectively, and (2) (
6.8
) holds on
\partial_{\rm so}{\mathsf{D}}
, and (3) with overwhelming probability,
the uniformly random height function
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}
of
{\mathsf{D}}
with boundary
{\mathsf{h}}
satisfies

(6.10)

\displaystyle|\widehat{\mathsf{H}}(nv)-nH^{*}(v)|\leq n^{{\mathfrak{d}}},\quad v\in\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}}\cap{\mathfrak{D}}.

Then, using
6.1
and the fact that the west and east boundaries of
{\mathfrak{D}}
either coincide with the boundary of
{\mathfrak{P}}
or are in the frozen region and bounded away from the liquid region, we see that: with overwhelming probability, the restriction of
{\mathsf{H}}
on
\partial{\mathsf{D}}
is in
\Omega
.

In the rest of the proof, we fix a
{\mathsf{h}}\in\Omega
, and denote by
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}
the uniformly random height function
{\mathsf{D}}
with boundary
{\mathsf{h}}
.
By
6.4
, there is a random plausible boundary height function
\widetilde{\mathsf{h}}
of
{\mathsf{D}}
, such that (1)
\widetilde{\mathsf{h}}={\mathsf{h}}
on
\partial_{\we}{\mathsf{D}}\cup\partial_{\ea}{\mathsf{D}}\cup\partial_{\so}{\mathsf{D}}
,
and (2) if
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
is a uniformly random height function of
\mathsf{D}
with boundary
\widetilde{\mathsf{h}}
, then with overwhelming probability, (
6.9
) holds for any
{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\leq t\leq{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}
.

We consider a small box around the cusp location:
{\mathfrak{B}}:=[x_{c}-{\mathfrak{c}},x_{c}+{\mathfrak{c}}]\times[{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}=t_{c}-{\mathfrak{s}},{\mathfrak{t}}_{1}=t_{c}+{\mathfrak{s}}]
with
{\mathfrak{c}}>0
being a small constant.
By taking
{\mathfrak{c}}
small and then
{\mathfrak{s}}
small, we can ensure that
{\mathfrak{B}}\subset{\mathfrak{D}}
and the west, north, and east boundaries of
{\mathfrak{B}}
are all in the liquid region.
In particular, we have
x_{c}-{\mathfrak{c}}\leq E_{-}(t)
and
x_{c}+{\mathfrak{c}}\geq E_{+}(t)
for each
{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\leq t\leq t_{c}
.
We next show that with overwhelming
probability, for any
(x,t)\in\partial{\mathfrak{B}}
,

(6.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}(nx-n^{4{\mathfrak{d}}},nt)\leq\widehat{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt),\quad(t,x)\in\partial{\mathfrak{B}}.\end{split}

For the south boundary, since
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}(nx,n{\mathfrak{t}}_{0})=\widehat{\mathsf{H}}(nx,n{\mathfrak{t}}_{0})={\mathsf{h}}(nx,{\mathfrak{t}}_{0})
for
x\in[x_{c}-{\mathfrak{c}},x_{c}+{\mathfrak{c}}]
,
(
6.11
) follows trivially from the monotonicity of
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
. For
(x,t)\in\partial{\mathfrak{B}}\setminus\partial_{\mathrm{s}o}{\mathfrak{D}}
, it is in the liquid region, so
\partial_{x}H^{*}
is bounded away from
0
and
1
in a neighborhood of
(x,t)
.
Therefore,

(6.12)

\displaystyle H^{*}(x,t)-H^{*}(x-n^{4{\mathfrak{d}}-1},t)\geq cn^{4{\mathfrak{d}}-1},\quad H^{*}(x+n^{4{\mathfrak{d}}-1},t)-H^{*}(x,t)\geq cn^{4{\mathfrak{d}}-1},

for some constant
c>0
. Combining (
6.9
) with (
6.12
), we then obtain that

\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)\geq nH^{*}(x,t)-n^{{\mathfrak{d}}}\geq nH^{*}(x-n^{4{\mathfrak{d}}-1},t)+cn^{4{\mathfrak{d}}}-n^{{\mathfrak{d}}}\geq\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}(nx-n^{4{\mathfrak{d}}},nt).

Now, given (
6.11
), using
3.3
, we can couple
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}
on
n{\mathfrak{B}}-(n^{4{\mathfrak{d}}},0)
with
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}
on
n{\mathfrak{B}}
such that

(6.13)

\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}(nx-n^{4{\mathfrak{d}}},nt)\leq\widehat{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)

for any
(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{B}}
. Thus, under the above assumption that
H^{*}\equiv 0
in
\{(x,t):{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\leq t\leq t_{c},x\in[E_{-}(t),E_{+}(t)]\}
,
(
6.9
) and (
6.13
) imply that with overwhelming probability,
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)\geq 0
for any
{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\leq t\leq t_{c}
and

(6.14)

x\in[E_{-}(t)+(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}n^{6{\mathfrak{d}}-2/3}+n^{-1+4{\mathfrak{d}}},E_{+}(t)-(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}n^{6{\mathfrak{d}}-2/3}-n^{-1+4{\mathfrak{d}}}].

With a similar inequality
\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}(nx+n^{4{\mathfrak{d}}},nt)\geq\widehat{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)
, we can show that with overwhelming probability,
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)\leq 0
for all such
t
and
x
, thereby concluding
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)=0
.
Recall that the interval in (
6.14
) is non-empty only when
t\leq t_{c}-n^{-1/2+4{\mathfrak{d}}}
, in which case the term
n^{-1+4{\mathfrak{d}}}
is negligible. Hence, we have obtained that
\widehat{\mathsf{H}}(nx,nt)=0
for any
{\mathfrak{t}}_{0}\leq t\leq t_{c}
and
x\in[E_{-}(t)+(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}n^{\delta-2/3},E_{+}(t)-(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}n^{\delta-2/3}]
as long as we take
\delta>6{\mathfrak{d}}
. Since
{\mathsf{h}}
is arbitrarily taken from
\Omega
, by Lemma
3.4
and the above fact that the restriction of
{\mathsf{H}}
on
\partial{\mathsf{D}}
is in
\Omega
with overwhelming probability, the conclusion follows.
∎

7.
Complex slope and proofs of some deterministic estimates

In this section, we analyze the limiting height function using the complex Burgers equation (
2.2
). Combining the obtained estimates with the optimal height function concentration results from
Section

6
, we will finish the proofs of the remaining statements in
Section

4
.

We work under the same setup as in
Section

4
.
More precisely, we fix a rational polygonal set
{\mathfrak{P}}
satisfying
2.5
and a cusp point
(x_{c},t_{c})\in{\mathfrak{A}}
, which is upward oriented as in
Definition

2.6
with curvature parameters
{\mathfrak{r}},{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Let
n
be a large integer such that
n{\mathfrak{P}}
is a tileable domain.
All the constants in this section may depend on
{\mathfrak{P}}
.

We denote
\Delta t=n^{-\omega}
for some constant
\omega\in(0,1/2)
, and take
t_{0}<t_{c}<t_{1}
, such that
t_{0},t_{1}\in n^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
and
t_{c}-t_{0},t_{1}-t_{c}\asymp\Delta t
.
Around
(x_{c},t_{c})
and between time
t_{0}
and
t_{1}
, the arctic curve
{\mathfrak{A}}
contains two analytic pieces
\{(E_{-}(t),t):t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{c}\}
and
\{(E_{+}(t),t):t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{c}\}
.
Let
{\mathfrak{c}}>0
be a small enough constant depending on
{\mathfrak{P}}
and
(x_{c},t_{c})
. Then,
M,N\in\mathbb{N}
are defined such that

[\![{-M,N}]\!]=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:H^{*}(x_{c}-{\mathfrak{c}},t_{0})\leq H^{*}(x_{c},t_{c})+i/n<H^{*}(x_{c}+{\mathfrak{c}},t_{0})\},

where
H^{*}
is the limiting height function. We denote the density
\rho^{*}_{t}(x)=\partial_{x}H^{*}(x,t)
, which is defined almost everywhere and takes values in
[0,1]
since
H^{*}
is admissible.

Besides the setup in
Section

4
,
we further assume that
H^{*}(x,t)=0
for
t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{c}
and
E_{-}(t)\leq x\leq E_{+}(t)
. Then, the
\rho^{*}_{t}
quantiles
\gamma_{i}(t)
are defined through the relation
H^{*}(\gamma_{i}(t),t)=i/n
(and
\gamma_{0}(t)
is chosen to equal
E_{+}(t)
).
We also denote
c(t)=x_{c}+(t-t_{c})/{\mathfrak{r}}
.

7.1.
Density estimate: Proofs of Lemmas
4.1
and
4.2

We start with the following estimate of the density
\rho_{t}^{*}
in a neighborhood of the cusp location
(x_{c},t_{c})
.

Proposition 7.1
.

The followings hold for a sufficiently small constant
c_{0}>0
and arbitrarily large
C>0
:

(1)

For
t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{c}
, we have
E_{+}(t)-E_{-}(t)\asymp(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}
. For
0\leq x-E_{+}(t)\leq C(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}
, we have

(7.1)

\displaystyle\rho_{t}^{*}(x)=\frac{\sqrt{C_{*}(x-E_{+}(t))}}{(t_{c}-t)^{1/4}}+\OO\left((t_{c}-t)^{1/4}|x-E_{+}(t)|^{1/2}+\frac{|x-E_{+}(t)|}{t_{c}-t}\right),

where
C_{*}>0
is a constant.
For
C(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}\leq x-E_{+}(t)\leq c_{0}
, we have
\rho_{t}^{*}(x)\asymp|x-c(t)|^{1/3}
.
Analogous statements hold for
0\leq E_{-}(t)-x\leq c_{0}
.

(2)

For
t_{c}<t\leq t_{1}
, we have
\rho_{t}^{*}(x)\asymp(t-t_{c})^{1/2}\vee|x-c(t)|^{1/3}
when
|x-c(t)|\leq c_{0}
.

Part (1) of this lemma has been proved in
[
Hua21
, Proposition 3.3]
.
The proof of part (2) will be given in
Section

7.4
.

The following lemma gives an estimate on
\gamma_{0}(t)
, which will be proved in
Section

7.6
.

Lemma 7.2
.

There exists a constant
{\mathfrak{C}}>0
such that for any
t_{c}<t\leq t_{1}
,
|\gamma_{0}(t)-c(t)|\leq{\mathfrak{C}}(t-t_{c})^{3/2}
.

Combining
7.1
and
7.2
, we readily conclude the proof of
4.1
.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

Recall that
t_{c}-t_{0}\asymp\Delta t=n^{-\omega}
with
\omega\in(0,1/2)
.
For
1\leq i\lesssim\Delta t^{2}n
, we can integrate (
7.1
) to get

(7.2)

\displaystyle\frac{i}{n}=\int^{\gamma_{i}(t_{0})}_{E_{+}(t_{0})}\rho_{t_{0}}^{*}(x){\rm d}x\asymp\frac{(\gamma_{i}(t_{0})-E_{+}(t_{0}))^{3/2}}{\Delta t^{1/4}},

which yields
\gamma_{i}(t_{0})-E_{+}(t_{0})\asymp\Delta t^{1/6}(i/n)^{2/3}
. This gives the first relation in (
4.2
). The second one follows similarly.

Next, we prove the first relation in (
4.3
), and the second relation can be proven in the same way. We recall that
c(t)=x_{c}+(t-t_{c})/{\mathfrak{r}}\in[E_{-}(t),E_{+}(t)]
. For
t\leq t_{c}
, similar to (
7.2
), using Item 1 of
7.1
, we have that for
i\gtrsim\Delta t^{2}n
,

(7.3)

\displaystyle\frac{i}{n}=\int^{\gamma_{i}(t)}_{E_{+}(t)}\rho_{t}^{*}(x){\rm d}x\asymp(\gamma_{i}(t)-c(t))^{4/3},

which concludes that
\gamma_{i}(t)-c(t)\asymp(i/n)^{3/4}
.

For
t\geq t_{c}
, by
7.2
and Item 2 of
7.1
, for any
x\geq c(t)+2{\mathfrak{C}}(t-t_{c})^{3/2}
we have

\displaystyle\int^{x}_{\gamma_{0}(t)}\rho_{t}^{*}(y){\rm d}y\leq\int^{x}_{c(t)-{\mathfrak{C}}\Delta t^{3/2}}\rho_{t}^{*}(y){\rm d}y\lesssim\int^{x}_{c(t)-{\mathfrak{C}}\Delta t^{3/2}}(t-t_{c})^{1/2}\vee|y-c(t)|^{1/3}{\rm d}y\lesssim(x-c(t))^{4/3},

\displaystyle\int^{x}_{\gamma_{0}(t)}\rho_{t}^{*}(y){\rm d}y\geq\int^{x}_{c(t)+{\mathfrak{C}}\Delta t^{3/2}}\rho_{t}^{*}(y){\rm d}y\gtrsim\int^{x}_{c(t)+{\mathfrak{C}}\Delta t^{3/2}}(t-t_{c})^{1/2}\vee|y-c(t)|^{1/3}{\rm d}y\gtrsim(x-c(t))^{4/3}.

Namely, we have
\int^{x}_{\gamma_{0}(t)}\rho_{t}^{*}(y){\rm d}y\asymp(x-c(t))^{4/3}
for
x\geq c(t)+2{\mathfrak{C}}(t-t_{c})^{3/2}
.
This implies that
\gamma_{i}(t)-c(t)\asymp(i/n)^{3/4}
when
i\geq C\Delta t^{2}n
for some sufficiently large constant
C
.
This finishes the proof of (
4.3
).
∎

For
4.2
, we also need to use the optimal rigidity proved in
Section

6
.

Proof of
4.2
.

By
6.1
, with overwhelming probability, for any
x
and
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]\cap n^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
,

(7.4)

\displaystyle|\{i\geq 0:{\mathsf{q}}_{i}(nt)\leq nx\}|=nH^{*}(x,t)+\OO(n^{{\mathfrak{d}}}).

It then follows that there is a sufficiently large constant
S>0
, such that for any
j\geq 0
,

(7.5)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{q}}_{0}(nt)/n\vee\gamma_{j-\lfloor Sn^{{\mathfrak{d}}}\rfloor}(t)\leq{\mathsf{q}}_{j}(nt)/n\leq\gamma_{j+\lfloor Sn^{{\mathfrak{d}}}\rfloor}(t).

By
6.2
, with overwhelming probability, we have
{\mathsf{H}}(nv)=nH^{*}(v)
for
v=(x,t)
with
t\in[t_{0},t_{c}]
and
x\in[E_{-}(t)+(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}n^{{\mathfrak{d}}-2/3},E_{+}(t)-(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}n^{{\mathfrak{d}}-2/3}]
. We then have
E_{+}(t)-(t_{c}-t)^{1/6}n^{{\mathfrak{d}}-2/3}\leq{\mathsf{q}}_{0}(nt)
, which, together with (
7.5
) and
4.1
, gives

\displaystyle E_{+}(t_{0})-n^{-2/3+{\mathfrak{d}}}(t_{c}-t_{0})^{1/6}\leq{\mathsf{q}}_{0}(nt_{0})/n\leq\gamma_{\lfloor Sn^{{\mathfrak{d}}}\rfloor}(t_{0})\leq E_{+}(t_{0})+n^{-2/3+{\mathfrak{d}}}\Delta t^{1/6}.

Thus, we conclude that
{\mathsf{q}}_{0}(nt_{0})/n-E_{+}(t_{0})\lesssim n^{-2/3+{\mathfrak{d}}}\Delta t^{1/6}
.
A similar argument leads to the bound for
{\mathsf{q}}_{-1}(nt_{0})
and concludes (
4.4
).
The statement (
4.5
) is a consequence of (
7.4
), by noticing that

\displaystyle|\{i\in\llbracket-M,N\rrbracket:{\mathsf{q}}_{i}(nt_{0})<xn\}|={\mathsf{H}}(x,t_{0})+M+\OO(1),

\displaystyle|\{i\in\llbracket-M,N\rrbracket:\gamma_{i}(t_{0})<x\}|=nH(x,t_{0})+M+\OO(1).

We next prove the first estimate of (
4.6
).
Recall
L=\lceil n^{1+\delta}\Delta t^{2}\rceil\gg\Delta t^{2}n
. Using (
7.5
), (
4.3
), and Item 1 of
7.1
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathsf{q}}_{L}(nt)/n-\gamma_{L}(t)\leq\gamma_{L+\lfloor Sn^{{\mathfrak{d}}}\rfloor}(t)-\gamma_{L}(t)\lesssim\frac{n^{{\mathfrak{d}}}}{n\rho^{*}_{t}(\gamma_{L}(t))}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{n^{{\mathfrak{d}}}}{n^{3/4}L^{1/4}},

\displaystyle\gamma_{L}(t)-{\mathsf{q}}_{L}(nt)/n\leq\gamma_{L}(t)-\gamma_{L-\lfloor Sn^{{\mathfrak{d}}}\rfloor}(t)\lesssim\frac{n^{{\mathfrak{d}}}}{n\rho^{*}_{t}(\gamma_{L-\lfloor Sn^{{\mathfrak{d}}}\rfloor}(t))}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{n^{{\mathfrak{d}}}}{n^{3/4}|L-\lfloor Sn^{{\mathfrak{d}}}\rfloor|^{1/4}}.

Then, using that
L\gg n^{\delta}
and choosing
{\mathfrak{d}}
sufficiently small depending on
\delta
, we conclude that
{\mathsf{q}}_{L}(nt)/n-\gamma_{L}(t)\lesssim n^{-3/4-{\mathfrak{d}}}
for any
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]\cap n^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
.
The proofs of the second estimate of (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) are similar.
∎

7.2.
Complex slope revisit

Recall (from
Section

2.3
) the complex slope
f^{*}_{t}(x)
for
(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{L}}
.
Consider a box around the cusp location
(x_{c},t_{c})
as
{\mathfrak{B}}=[x_{c}-{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime},x_{c}+{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}]\times[t_{c}-{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime},t_{c}+{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}]
for a small enough constant
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}>0
.
We denote
\bar{t}_{0}=t_{c}-{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}
and
\bar{t}_{1}=t_{c}+{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}
.
We can take
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}
small enough such that the complex slope on the liquid region
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{B}})={\mathfrak{L}}\cap{\mathfrak{B}}
can be reparametrized as an analytic function.
Such a reparametrization has been done in
[
Hua21
]
, as summarized in the following proposition.

Proposition 7.3
.

There exists a small enough constant
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}>0
such that the followings hold:

(1)

For any
t\in[\bar{t}_{0},\bar{t}_{1}]
, let
{\mathfrak{B}}^{t}=\{(x,s)\in{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{B}}):t\leq s\leq\bar{t}_{1}\}
. The following map

(7.6)

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}:(x,s)\in{\mathfrak{B}}^{t}\mapsto x+(t-s)\frac{f^{*}_{s}(x)}{f^{*}_{s}(x)+1}\in\mathbb{H}\cup\mathbb{R}

is a bijection to its image.
In addition,
(x,s)\mapsto f^{*}_{s}(x)
can be continuously extended to the boundary of
{\mathfrak{B}}^{t}
, therefore (
7.6
) can also be continuously extended to the boundary of
{\mathfrak{B}}^{t}
. It maps the north, west, and east boundaries of
{\mathfrak{B}}^{t}
,
\partial_{\north}{\mathfrak{B}}^{t}\cup\partial_{\we}{\mathfrak{B}}^{t}\cup\partial_{\ea}{\mathfrak{B}}^{t}
, to a curve in the upper half-plane, and the remaining boundary of
{\mathfrak{B}}^{t}
to an interval in
\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore, (
7.6
) and its complex conjugate together give a bijection from two copies of
{\mathfrak{B}}^{t}
, glued along the arctic curve, to a symmetric domain
\mathscr{U}_{t}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
.

(2)

The complex slope induces a family of analytic functions
f_{t}:\mathscr{U}_{t}\cap\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}^{-}
for
t\in[\bar{t}_{0},\bar{t}_{1}]
, satisfying the following relation:

(7.7)

\displaystyle f_{t}\left(\varphi_{t}(x,s)\right)=f_{s}^{*}(x),\quad(x,s)\in{\mathfrak{B}}^{t}.

In particular, for
s=t
we have
f_{t}(x)=f_{t}^{*}(x)
.
On
\mathscr{U}_{t}\cap\mathbb{H}
,
f_{t}
satisfies the complex Burgers equation

(7.8)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f_{t}(z)+\partial_{z}f_{t}(z)\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}=0.

(3)

Recall the density
\rho^{*}_{t}(x)=\partial_{x}H^{*}(x,t)
defined in
Section

4
, and denote its Stieltjes transform as

(7.9)

\displaystyle m_{t}^{*}(z)=\int\frac{\rho_{t}^{*}(x){\rm d}x}{z-x}.

Then
f_{t}
can be extended to the whole domain
\mathscr{U}_{t}
, and we have the decomposition

(7.10)

\displaystyle f_{t}(z)=e^{m_{t}^{*}(z)+g_{t}(z)},

where
g_{t}
is a real analytic function on
{\mathscr{U}}_{t}
.
1
1

1

A function
g
defined on a subset of
{\mathbb{C}}
is called real analytic if it is analytic and satisfies
\overline{g(z)}=g(\overline{z})
.

(4)

When
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}
sufficiently small, there is a one-variable real analytic function
Q
, such that for any
(x,t)
in the closure of
{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{B}})
,

(7.11)

Q(f^{*}_{t}(x))=x\big(f^{*}_{t}(x)+1\big)-tf^{*}_{t}(x).

Also, for any
(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{B}}
,
(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{A}}({\mathfrak{B}})={\mathfrak{A}}\cap{\mathfrak{B}}
if and only if
f_{t}(x)
is a double root of
f\mapsto Q(f)-x(f+1)+tf
, except for that
f_{t_{c}}(x_{c})=f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c})
is a triple root (but not a quadruple root) of
f\mapsto Q(f)-x_{c}(f+1)+t_{c}f
.

The first and third items follow from
[
Hua21
, Proposition 3.4]
.
The second item follows from
[
Hua21
, Proposition 3.1]
. The last item follows from
[
Hua21
, Proposition A.2]
, and the classification of singular points (see the discussion
at the end of
[
KO07
, Section 1.6]
).

The complex Burgers equation (
7.8
) can be solved readily using the characteristic flow. Fix any time
t\in[\bar{t}_{0},\bar{t}_{1}]
and
u\in\mathscr{U}_{t}
, we have that for
s\in[t,\bar{t}_{1}]
,

(7.12)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}f_{s}(z_{s}(u))=0,\quad\partial_{s}z_{s}(u)=\displaystyle\frac{f_{s}(z_{s}(u))}{f_{s}(z_{s}(u))+1}=\frac{f_{t}(u)}{f_{t}(u)+1},\quad z_{t}(u)=u.

The characteristic flow maps the sub-region

\{u\in\mathscr{U}_{t}\cap\mathbb{H}:\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}u\geq-(s-t)\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[f_{t}(u)/(f_{t}(u)+1)]\}
bijectively to
\mathscr{U}_{s}\cap\mathbb{H}
.
It then follows that
f_{s}
satisfies

(7.13)

\displaystyle f_{s}\left(u+(s-t)\frac{f_{t}(u)}{f_{t}(u)+1}\right)=f_{t}(u),\quad\bar{t}_{0}\leq t\leq s\leq\bar{t}_{1},\quad u\in\mathscr{U}_{t}.

For simplicity of notations, we introduce

w_{t}(z):=f_{t}(z)/(f_{t}(z)+1)
.
Then (
7.13
) can be rewritten as

(7.14)

w_{s}\left(z+(s-t)w_{t}(z)\right)=w_{t}(z).

Performing Taylor expansion of
Q
around
f_{t_{c}}^{*}(x_{c})
and using (
7.14
), we can show that
w_{t}(z)
satisfies the following equation (
7.15
).

Lemma 7.4
.

For any
t\in[\bar{t}_{0},\bar{t}_{1}]
and
z\in\mathscr{U}_{t}
, we have

(7.15)

z-x_{c}+(t_{c}-t)w_{t}(z)=\frac{a}{3}(w_{t}(z)-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))^{3}+\mathcal{E}(w_{t}(z)-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})),

where
w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})=\mathfrak{r}^{-1}\in(0,1)
,
a=\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}^{5}}{2({\mathfrak{r}}-1)^{5}}Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))
is a positive constant, and
\mathcal{E}
is an analytic function in a neighborhood around
0
satisfying
\mathcal{E}(w)=\OO(|w|^{4})
.

Proof.

Recall that the slope
\mathfrak{r}
of the tangent line through
(x_{c},t_{c})
is in
(1,\infty)
. Together with (
2.10
), it implies that
w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})=\mathfrak{r}^{-1}\in(0,1)
and
f_{t_{c}}^{*}(x_{c})=({\mathfrak{r}}-1)^{-1}\in(0,\infty)
. Hence, as long as
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}
is chosen sufficiently small, we have
f_{t_{c}}(z)
(resp.
w_{t}(z)
) for
z\in\mathscr{U}_{t_{c}}
is away from
\{-1,0,\infty\}
(resp.
\{0,1,\infty\}
) by a distance of order 1.

By
Item

4
of
7.3
, (
7.11
) holds for any
(x,t)\in\overline{{\mathfrak{L}}({\mathfrak{B}})}
, and

(7.16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Q(f_{t_{c}}^{*}(x_{c}))=x_{c}\big(f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c})+1\big)-t_{c}f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}),\\
&Q^{\prime}(f_{t_{c}}^{*}(x_{c}))=x_{c}-t_{c},\quad Q^{\prime\prime}(f_{t_{c}}^{*}(x_{c}))=0,\quad Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f_{t_{c}}^{*}(x_{c}))\neq 0.\end{split}

Next, with (
7.7
) and (
7.11
), we can derive that for
z\in\mathscr{U}_{t_{c}}
, the following equation holds:

Q(f_{t_{c}}(z))=z(f_{t_{c}}(z)+1)-t_{c}f_{t_{c}}(z).

Then, with (
7.16
), performing the Taylor expansion of
Q
at
f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c})
gives that

(7.17)

\left(z-x_{c}\right)\left[1+f_{t_{c}}(z)\right]=\frac{1}{6}{Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))}(f_{t_{c}}(z)-f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))^{3}+{\mathcal{E}_{2}(f_{t_{c}}(z)-f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))},

where
\mathcal{E}_{2}(w)=\OO(|w|^{4})
is an analytic function in a neighborhood around
0
.
We further write (
7.17
) as

(7.18)

z-x_{c}=\frac{a_{1}}{3}(f_{t_{c}}(z)-f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))^{3}+{\mathcal{E}_{1}(f_{t_{c}}(z)-f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))},

where
\mathcal{E}_{1}(w)=\OO(|w|^{4})
is the analytic function obtained in this expansion and

a_{1}:=\frac{1}{2}\frac{Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))}{1+f_{t_{c}}^{*}(x_{c})}=\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}-1}{2{\mathfrak{r}}}Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c})).

Recall that
f_{t}(z)
satisfies (
7.13
), which implies that

(7.19)

f_{t}\left(z\right)=f_{t_{c}}\left(z+(t_{c}-t)\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}\right).

By plugging
z
in (
7.18
) as
z+(t_{c}-t)f_{t}(z)/(f_{t}(z)+1)
, we get that

(7.20)

z-x_{c}+(t_{c}-t)\frac{f_{t}(z)}{f_{t}(z)+1}=\frac{a_{1}}{3}(f_{t}(z)-f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))^{3}+{\mathcal{E}_{1}(f_{t}(z)-f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))}.

Then, plugging
f_{t}=w_{t}/(1-w_{t})
into (
7.20
), we can deduce (
7.15
).

It remains to show that
a
is positive. In fact, as the cusp is upward oriented, for
t\in[t_{0},t_{c})
,
w_{t}(x)
is real for
x\in[E_{-}(t),E_{+}(t)]
.
By
Item

4
of
7.3
,
f_{t}(E_{\pm}(t))
are double roots of
f\mapsto Q(f)-E_{\pm}(t)(f+1)+tf
, so

Q^{\prime}\left(f_{t}(E_{\pm}(t))\right)=E_{\pm}(t)-t.

Then, performing the Taylor expansion of
Q^{\prime}
around
f_{t_{c}}^{*}(x_{c})
and using (
7.16
), we obtain that

x_{c}-t_{c}+\frac{1}{2}{Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))}(f_{t}(E_{\pm}(t))-f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))^{2}+\OO(|f_{t}(E_{\pm}(t))-f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c})|^{3})=E_{\pm}(t)-t.

Plugging into
f_{t}=w_{t}/(1-w_{t})
and using (
7.15
) with
z=E_{\pm}(t)
, we can rewrite this equation as

(7.21)

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}^{4}}{2({\mathfrak{r}}-1)^{4}}{Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))}(w-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))^{2}+\OO(|w-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})|^{3})=(t_{c}-t)\left(1-w\right)

for
w=w_{t}(E_{\pm}(t))
.
Writing the right-hand side as
1-w=1-{\mathfrak{r}}^{-1}-(w-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))
, we can reduce (
7.21
) to

(7.22)

a(w-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))^{2}+\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}}{{\mathfrak{r}}-1}(t_{c}-t)(w-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))-(t_{c}-t)=\OO(|w-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})|^{3}).

Note that as an equation for
w
, (
7.22
) has two real roots around
w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})
, i.e.,
w_{t}(E_{\pm}(t))
; but that only happens when
a>0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

7.3.
Matching the curvature parameters: Proof of
4.6

First, we notice that the two analytic pieces
E_{\pm}(t)
,
t\in[t_{0},t_{c}]
, near the cusp are determined by
a
as follows.

Lemma 7.5
.

For any
t\in[t_{0},t_{c}]
, we have

(7.23)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} E_{-}(t)=x_{c}-\frac{t_{c}-t}{\mathfrak{r}}-\frac{2(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}}{3\sqrt{a}}+\OO(|t_{c}-t|^{2}),\quad&w_{t}(E_{-}(t))=w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})+\sqrt{\frac{t_{c}-t}{a}}+\OO(|t_{c}-t|),\\
E_{+}(t)=x_{c}-\frac{t_{c}-t}{\mathfrak{r}}+\frac{2(t_{c}-t)^{3/2}}{3\sqrt{a}}+\OO(|t_{c}-t|^{2}),\quad&w_{t}(E_{+}(t))=w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})-\sqrt{\frac{t_{c}-t}{a}}+\OO(|t_{c}-t|).\end{aligned}

Proof.

In the proof of
7.4
above, we have seen that
w=w_{t}(E_{\pm}(t))
satisfies (
7.22
).
Solving it, we get the estimates on
w_{t}(E_{\pm}(t))
. Plugging them further into (
7.15
), we obtain the estimates on
E_{\pm}(t)
.
∎

Comparing (
7.23
) with (
2.12
), we observe that

(7.24)

3{\mathfrak{q}}^{-2}=a=\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}^{5}}{({\mathfrak{r}}-1)^{5}}Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f^{*}_{t_{c}}(x_{c})).

The following lemma computes the derivatives of the complex slope
f_{t_{0}}
at
z_{c}=x_{c}-(t_{c}-t_{0})/{\mathfrak{r}}
, as defined in (
4.9
).
For the convenience of notations and easier comparison with
5.2
, for the rest of this section, we shift the domain
{\mathfrak{P}}
(by an amount depending on
n
) to assume that
t_{0}=0
. Note that
t_{c}
would then be
n
dependent with
t_{c}\asymp\Delta t
.

Lemma 7.6
.

We have

(7.25)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &f_{0}(z_{c})=({\mathfrak{r}}-1)^{-1},\quad f_{0}^{\prime}(z_{c})=-t_{c}^{-1}\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}^{2}}{({\mathfrak{r}}-1)^{2}},\quad f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})=2t_{c}^{-2}\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}^{3}}{({\mathfrak{r}}-1)^{3}},\\
&f_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(z_{c})=-t_{c}^{-4}\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}^{7}}{({\mathfrak{r}}-1)^{7}}Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f_{t_{c}}(x_{c}))-6t_{c}^{-3}\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}^{4}}{({\mathfrak{r}}-1)^{4}}.\end{aligned}

Proof.

First, by (
7.13
), we have
f_{0}(z_{c})=f_{t_{c}}(x_{c})=({\mathfrak{r}}-1)^{-1}
. Then, (
7.16
) gives that

(7.26)

Q^{\prime}(f_{0}(z_{c}))=x_{c}-t_{c},\quad Q^{\prime\prime}(f_{0}(z_{c}))=0,\quad Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f_{0}(z_{c}))\neq 0

Next, by the relation (
7.7
),

\displaystyle f_{0}(\varphi_{0}(x,t))=f_{t}^{*}(x),\quad\varphi_{0}(x,t)=x-t\frac{f_{t}^{*}(x)}{f_{t}^{*}(x)+1}.

Denoting
z=\varphi_{0}(x,t)
, and plugging the above line into (
7.11
), we get that

(7.27)

\displaystyle\frac{Q(f_{0}(z))}{f_{0}(z)+1}=x-t\frac{f_{t}^{*}(x)}{f_{t}^{*}(x)+1}=z\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ Q(f_{0}(z))=z(f_{0}(z)+1).

Taking the derivative of (
7.27
) with respect to
z
gives

(7.28)

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}(f_{0}(z))f_{0}^{\prime}(z)=f_{0}(z)+1+zf_{0}^{\prime}(z).

Plugging
z=z_{c}
into (
7.28
), using (
7.26
) and
z_{c}=x_{c}-t_{c}w_{0}(z_{c})
, we get

(7.29)

1+t_{c}\frac{f_{0}^{\prime}(z_{c})}{(f_{0}(z_{c})+1)^{2}}=0,

from which we can solve
f_{0}^{\prime}(z_{c})
.
Taking one more derivative of (
7.28
) with respect to
z
, we get

(7.30)

\displaystyle Q^{\prime\prime}(f_{0}(z))(f_{0}^{\prime}(z))^{2}+Q^{\prime}(f_{0}(z))f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z)=2f_{0}^{\prime}(z)+zf_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z).

Plugging
z=z_{c}
into (
7.30
) and using (
7.26
) and (
7.29
), we get

(7.31)

f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})-\frac{2f_{0}^{\prime}(z_{c})^{2}}{f_{0}(z_{c})+1}=0,

from which we can solve
f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})
.
Finally, taking another derivative of (
7.30
) with respect to
z
, we get

(7.32)

\displaystyle Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f_{0}(z))(f_{0}^{\prime}(z))^{3}+3Q^{\prime\prime}(f_{0}(z))f_{0}^{\prime}(z)f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z)+Q^{\prime}(f_{0}(z))f_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)=3f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z)+zf_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(z).

Plugging
z=z_{c}
into (
7.32
) and using (
7.26
), we get

\displaystyle(z_{c}-x_{c}+t_{c})f_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(z_{c})=Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f_{0}(z_{c}))(f_{0}^{\prime}(z_{c}))^{3}-3f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c}),

from which we can solve
f_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(z_{c})
.
∎

Now, we are ready to complete the proof of
4.6
with the above lemma and
4.3
.

Proof of
4.6
.

By
4.3
, we have
\widetilde{B}=(\widetilde{x}_{c}-\widetilde{z}_{c})/\widetilde{t}_{c}=\widetilde{w}_{0}(\widetilde{z}_{c})\to{\mathfrak{r}}^{-1}
, and that

\widetilde{t}_{c}+\widetilde{z}_{c}-\widetilde{x}_{c}=t_{c}+z_{c}-x_{c}+\OO(\Delta t^{2})=(1-{\mathfrak{r}}^{-1})t_{c}+\OO(\Delta t^{2})\asymp\Delta t,\quad\widetilde{x}_{c}-\widetilde{z}_{c}={\mathfrak{r}}^{-1}t_{c}+\OO(\Delta t^{2})\asymp\Delta t.

These two estimates show that the second and third terms in the definition of
\widetilde{A}
are of order
\OO(\Delta t)
.
Next, by (
7.23
), we have that

(7.33)

z_{c}\in(E_{-}(0),E_{+}(0)),\quad\text{with}\quad z_{c}-E_{-}(0),E_{+}(0)-z_{c}\asymp\Delta t^{3/2},

and a similar estimate holds for
\widetilde{z}_{c}=z_{c}+\OO(\Delta t^{2})
.
Then, combining (
7.33
) with (
5.6
), we get that

\int\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}(x)}{(\widetilde{z}_{c}-x)^{4}}{\rm d}x-\int\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{0}(x)}{(z_{c}-x)^{4}}{\rm d}x\lesssim\Delta t^{-11/2}\Delta t^{2}=\Delta t^{-7/2},

which is negligible under the scaling
\Delta t^{4}
.
Furthermore, using the fact that
z_{c}
is away from the support of
\rho_{0}^{*}-\widetilde{\rho}_{0}
, which is contained in
\mathbb{R}\setminus[\gamma_{-M}(0),\gamma_{N}(0)]
, by a distance of order 1, we easily get that

\widetilde{t}_{c}^{4}\int\frac{|\rho_{0}^{*}(x)-\widetilde{\rho}_{0}(x)|}{4(z_{c}-x)^{4}}{\rm d}x=\OO(\Delta t^{4}).

Combining the above facts and using that
{\widetilde{t}_{c}}/{t_{c}}=(t_{c}+\OO(\Delta t^{2}))/{t_{c}}\to 1
, we observe that to show the limit of
\widetilde{A}
, it suffices to prove

(7.34)

t_{c}^{4}\int\frac{\rho_{0}^{*}(x)}{4(z_{c}-x)^{4}}{\rm d}x=\frac{-t_{c}^{4}}{24}m^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}(z_{c})\to{\mathfrak{r}}^{2}({\mathfrak{r}}-1)^{-1}{\mathfrak{q}}^{-2}/4.

Using the decomposition (
7.10
) and that
g_{0}(z)
is real analytic, we can calculate that

(7.35)

\displaystyle m^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}(z_{c})=[\log f_{0}(z_{c})]^{\prime\prime\prime}+\OO(1)=\frac{f_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}(z_{c})}{f_{0}(z_{c})}-\frac{3f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})f_{0}^{\prime}(z_{c})}{f_{0}(z_{c})^{2}}+\frac{2f_{0}^{\prime}(z_{c})^{3}}{f_{0}(z_{c})^{3}}+\OO(1).

Plugging (
7.25
) into (
7.35
), we obtain that

(7.36)

\displaystyle-\frac{t_{c}^{4}}{24}m^{\prime\prime\prime}_{0}(z_{c})\to\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}^{7}}{24({\mathfrak{r}}-1)^{6}}Q^{\prime\prime\prime}(f_{t_{c}}(x_{c})).

Finally, plugging (
7.24
) into (
7.36
) concludes (
7.34
).
∎

7.4.
Density estimate: Proof of Item 2 of
7.1

By
Item

3
of
7.3
, we can recover the density
\rho^{*}_{t}
as

\rho^{*}_{t}(x)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\arg^{*}f_{t}(x)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\arg^{*}\frac{w_{t}(x)}{1-w_{t}(x)}.

For
w_{t}(x)
in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})\in(0,1)
, we have

(7.37)

\left|\frac{w_{t}(x)}{1-w_{t}(x)}\right|\gtrsim 1\quad\text{and}\quad-\mathrm{Im}\,\frac{w_{t}(x)}{1-w_{t}(x)}=\frac{-\mathrm{Im}\,w_{t}(x)}{|1-w_{t}(x)|^{2}},

which gives that
\rho_{t}(x)\asymp-\mathrm{Im}\,w_{t}(x)
. Hence, to prove Item 2 in
7.1
, we only need to estimate the order of
\mathrm{Im}\,w_{t}(x)
. For simplicity of notations, given
x,t
with
t_{c}<t\leq t_{1}
and
|x-c(t)|\leq c_{0}
, we denote

\varpi:=w_{t}(x)-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c}),\quad\tau:=\frac{t-t_{c}}{a},\quad y:=\frac{3(x-c(t))}{2a},\quad\varepsilon(\varpi):=\frac{3\mathcal{E}(\varpi)}{2a},

where
\mathcal{E}
is from
7.4
and
\mathcal{E}(\varpi)=\OO(|\varpi|^{4})
.
Then we can rewrite the equation (
7.15
) as

(7.38)

\varpi^{3}+3\tau\varpi+2\varepsilon(\varpi)=2y.

Using the general cubic formula, we obtain that

(7.39)

\varpi=\alpha\left[(y-\varepsilon(\varpi))+\sqrt{(y-\varepsilon(\varpi))^{2}+\tau^{3}}\right]^{1/3}+\alpha^{-1}\left[(y-\varepsilon(\varpi))-\sqrt{(y-\varepsilon(\varpi))^{2}+\tau^{3}}\right]^{1/3},

where
\alpha
is a primitive cube root of unity chosen such that
\mathrm{Im}\,\varpi<0
. Here (and also for the rest of this paper) we use the convention that
z^{1/3}\in\mathbb{R}
for
z\in\mathbb{R}
.

We first consider the case
|y|\leq C_{0}\tau^{3/2}
for a large enough constant
C_{0}>0
. From equation (
7.38
), we obtain that
\varpi=\OO(\tau^{1/2})
. Then, we can expand (
7.39
) as

\varpi=\alpha\left[y+\sqrt{y^{2}+\tau^{3}}+\OO(\tau^{2})\right]^{1/3}+\alpha^{-1}\left[y-\sqrt{y^{2}+\tau^{3}}+\OO(\tau^{2})\right]^{1/3}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Im}\,\varpi

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\left[y+\sqrt{y^{2}+\tau^{3}}+\OO(\tau^{2})\right]^{1/3}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\left[y-\sqrt{y^{2}+\tau^{3}}+\OO(\tau^{2})\right]^{1/3}\asymp\tau^{1/2}.

Therefore, we conclude that when
3|x-c(t)|/2a=|y|\leq C_{0}\tau^{3/2}\asymp(t-t_{c})^{3/2}
, we have

(7.40)

\displaystyle\rho_{t}(x)\asymp-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}w_{t}(x)=-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\varpi\asymp\tau^{1/2}\asymp(t-t_{c})^{1/2}.

Next, consider the case
C_{0}\tau^{3/2}\leq|y|\leq c_{0}
. From equation (
7.38
), we obtain that
\varpi=\OO(|y|^{1/3})
, with which we can expand (
7.39
) as

\varpi=\alpha\left[y+|y|+\OO\left(|y|^{4/3}+\frac{\tau^{3}}{|y|}\right)\right]^{1/3}+\alpha^{-1}\left[y-|y|+\OO\left(|y|^{4/3}+\frac{\tau^{3}}{|y|}\right)\right]^{1/3}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Im}\,\varpi

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\left[2|y|+\OO\left(|y|^{4/3}+\frac{\tau^{3}}{|y|}\right)\right]^{1/3}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\left[\OO\left(|y|^{4/3}+\frac{\tau^{3}}{|y|}\right)\right]^{1/3}\asymp|y|^{1/3}.

Therefore, we conclude that when
c_{0}\geq 3|x-c(t)|/2a=|y|\geq C_{0}\tau^{3/2}\asymp(t-t_{c})^{3/2}
, we have

(7.41)

\displaystyle\rho_{t}(x)\asymp-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}w_{t}(x)=-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\varpi\asymp|y|^{1/3}\asymp|x-x_{c}|^{1/3}

Item 2 of
7.1
follows from combining (
7.40
) and (
7.41
).

7.5.
NBRW estimates: Proofs of
4.3
and
4.4

We first show that the complex slope corresponding to the limit shape of an NBRW also solves a complex Burgers equation.

Proposition 7.7
.

Take any
\beta\in(0,1)
and a density
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
. There exists a process
\{\widetilde{\rho}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
with Stieltjes transform

(7.42)

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{t}(z):=\int\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{t}(x){\rm d}x}{z-x},\quad\widetilde{f}_{t}(z):=\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}e^{\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)},

which solves the complex Burgers equation

(7.43)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)+\partial_{z}\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)\frac{\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)}{\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)+1}=0,\quad z\in\mathbb{H}.

Proof.

We recall the free convolution with the semicircle law from random matrix theory. The
semicircle distribution
is described by the density

\varrho_{{\rm sc}}(x)=\sqrt{4-x^{2}}/(2\pi)\cdot\mathds{1}_{x\in[-2,2]}
.
For any
t>0
, we denote the rescaled semicircle density as
\varrho_{{\rm sc}}^{(t)}(x):=t^{-1/2}\varrho_{{\rm sc}}(t^{-1/2}x)
. Given a positive measure
\nu
, the free convolution
\nu_{t}:=\nu\boxplus\varrho_{{\rm sc}}^{(t)}
of
\nu
with
\varrho_{{\rm sc}}^{(t)}
is characterized by its Stieltjes transform

\chi_{t}(z)=\int\frac{{\rm d}\nu_{t}(x)}{z-x},

which satisfies the equation

(7.44)

\displaystyle\chi_{t}(z+t\chi_{0}(z))=\chi_{0}(z).

The complex Burgers equation (
7.43
) can be solved using characteristic flow as

(7.45)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{t}\left(z+t\frac{\widetilde{f}_{0}(z)}{\widetilde{f}_{0}(z)+1}\right)=\widetilde{f}_{0}(z).

Now, we define

(7.46)

\displaystyle\chi_{0}(z):=\frac{\widetilde{f}_{0}(z)}{\widetilde{f}_{0}(z)+1}-\beta.

Then, for
z\in\mathbb{H}
, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)]\in(-\pi,0)
, and

(7.47)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\chi_{0}(z)]=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\widetilde{f}_{0}(z)}{|\widetilde{f}_{0}(z)+1|^{2}}=\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\frac{|e^{\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)}|\cdot\mathrm{Im}\,e^{\mathrm{i}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)]}}{|\widetilde{f}_{0}(z)+1|^{2}}<0.

Moreover, by our construction,
\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\chi_{0}(z)=0
.
Hence, by the Nevanlinna representation, there exists a positive measure
\nu
such that
\chi_{0}(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of
\nu
.
Then, we can construct
\chi_{t}(z)
as in (
7.44
), which is the Stieltjes transform of
\nu_{t}=\nu\boxplus\varrho_{{\rm sc}}^{(t)}
. Once we have constructed
\chi_{t}(z)
, we let

(7.48)

\displaystyle\chi_{t}(z)=\frac{\widetilde{f}_{t}(z+\beta t)}{\widetilde{f}_{t}(z+\beta t)+1}-\beta,\quad\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)=\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}e^{\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)}=\frac{\chi_{t}(z-\beta t)+\beta}{1-\beta-\chi_{t}(z-\beta t)}.

With (
7.44
), we can readily check that
\widetilde{f}_{t}
satisfies the complex Burgers equation (
7.45
).
For
z\in\mathbb{H}
, we have
\chi_{t}(z)\in\mathbb{H}^{-}
, thus the above construction gives
e^{\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)}\in\mathbb{H}^{-}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)]\in(-\pi,0)
. Moreover, we have
\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)=0
.
Using the Nevanlinna representation again, there exists a density
\widetilde{\rho}_{t}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
such that

\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)=\int\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{t}(x){\rm d}x}{z-x}.

This gives the construction of the process
\{\widetilde{\rho}_{t}(x)\}_{t\geq 0}
.
∎

For the convenience of notations, in the rest of this section, we also shift the domain
{\mathfrak{P}}
by an amount depending on
n
such that
x_{c}=(t_{c}-t_{0})/{\mathfrak{r}}=t_{c}/{\mathfrak{r}}
.
Then,
x_{c}
would be
n
dependent and
z_{c}=0
from (
4.9
).

Below we take
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}=\widetilde{\rho}_{t_{0}}
from (
4.8
), and let
\widetilde{\rho}_{t}
,
\widetilde{m}_{t}
,
\widetilde{f}_{t}
for
0\leq t\leq t_{1}
be given by
7.7
. Denote
\widetilde{w}_{t}(z):={\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)}/({\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)+1})
.
Take
\beta
from (
4.10
). Then, we have

(7.49)

\widetilde{f}_{0}(0)=f_{t_{c}}(x_{c})=f_{0}\left(x_{c}-t_{c}w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})\right)=f_{0}(0),

where we used (
7.19
) in the second equality and
w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})=\mathfrak{r}^{-1}
in the third equality.

We denote
\Delta w_{0}(z)=\widetilde{w}_{0}(z)-w_{0}(z)
for
z\in\mathscr{U}_{0}
.
We claim that

(7.50)

\Delta w_{0}(z)=\OO(\left|z\right|).

For the proof of this claim, notice that
g_{0}(z)
in the decomposition (
7.10
) is a real analytic function, so
g_{0}(z)-g_{0}(0)\lesssim|z|
. Furthermore,

\left|\left[\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)-m_{0}^{*}(z)\right]-\left[\widetilde{m}_{0}(0)-m_{0}^{*}(0)\right]\right|\leq\int_{x\notin[\gamma_{-M}(0),\gamma_{N}(0)]}\left|\frac{1}{x-z}-\frac{1}{x}\right|\rho_{0}^{*}(x){\rm d}x\lesssim|z|,

where we used that
x,x-z\gtrsim 1
for
x\notin[\gamma_{-M}(0),\gamma_{N}(0)]
and
z\in\mathscr{U}_{0}
as long as
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}
is chosen sufficiently small depending on
{\mathfrak{c}}
. Thus, from (
7.49
) and (
7.10
), we derive that

(7.51)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{0}(z)=f_{0}(0)e^{\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{0}(0)}=f_{0}(z)e^{\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{0}(0)-(m_{0}^{*}(z)-m_{0}^{*}(0))-(g_{0}(z)-g_{0}(0))}=f_{0}(z)\left(1+\mathfrak{f}_{0}(z)\right),

where
\mathfrak{f}_{0}(z)
is an analytic function around 0 defined as

\mathfrak{f}_{0}(z)=e^{\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{0}(0)-(m_{0}^{*}(z)-m_{0}^{*}(0))-(g_{0}(z)-g_{0}(0))}-1=\OO(|z|).

With the above two equations, we conclude that

(7.52)

\displaystyle\Delta w_{0}(z)=\frac{\widetilde{f}_{0}(z)}{\widetilde{f}_{0}(z)+1}-\frac{f_{0}(z)}{f_{0}(z)+1}=\frac{\widetilde{f}_{0}(z)-f_{0}(z)}{(f_{0}(z)+1)(\widetilde{f}_{0}(z)+1)}=\frac{w_{0}(z)(1-w_{0}(z))\mathfrak{f}_{0}(z)}{w_{0}(z)\mathfrak{f}_{0}(z)+1}=\OO(|z|),

where for the last step we used that
w_{0}(z)
is bounded.

7.5.1.
Proof of
4.3

We first prove the estimate for
\widetilde{t}_{c}-t_{c}
. Recall from
5.2
that there is an a priori estimate
\widetilde{t}_{c}=\widetilde{t}_{c}-t_{0}\asymp\Delta t
.
We denote the two edges of
\widetilde{\rho}_{t}
for
0\leq t\leq\widetilde{t}_{c}
as
\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)
, so that
\widetilde{\rho}_{t}(x)=0
for
x\in[\widetilde{E}_{-}(t),\widetilde{E}_{+}(t)]
.
It is known from classical Stieltjes transform theory that
\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)
are characterized as the points
x\in\mathbb{R}
where
\widetilde{m}_{t}^{\prime}(x)
diverges, which, by the definitions of
\widetilde{f}_{t}
and
\widetilde{w}_{t}
in (
7.42
), implies that

1/\widetilde{w}_{t}^{\prime}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t))=0.

Similar to (
7.14
), from equation (
7.43
), we obtain that
\widetilde{w}_{t}(z)=\widetilde{w}_{0}(z-t\widetilde{w}_{t}(z))
for
t\in[0,t_{1}]
and
z\in\mathscr{U}_{t}
. Then, the implicit differentiation with respect to
z
yields that

[{\widetilde{w}_{t}^{\prime}(z)}]^{-1}=[\widetilde{w}_{0}^{\prime}(z-t\widetilde{w}_{t}(z))]^{-1}+t,

so
\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)
satisfy the equation

(7.53)

\frac{1}{\widetilde{w}_{0}^{\prime}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)-t\widetilde{w}_{t}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)))}+t=0.

Furthermore, with the definition of
\Delta w_{0}
in (
7.52
), we can calculate that

\widetilde{w}_{0}^{\prime}(z)=w_{0}^{\prime}(z)+\Delta w_{0}^{\prime}(z)=w_{0}^{\prime}(z)+\OO(|w_{0}^{\prime}(z)\mathfrak{f}_{0}(z)|+|\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{\prime}(z)|)=w_{0}^{\prime}(z)(1+\OO(|z|))+\OO(1).

Plugging it into (
7.53
), we get that

(7.54)

\frac{1}{w_{0}^{\prime}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)-t\widetilde{w}_{t}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)))}+t+\OO\left(t^{2}+t|\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)-t\widetilde{w}_{t}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t))|\right)=0.

For simplicity of notations, we rewrite equation (
7.15
) with
t=0
and
x_{c}=t_{c}/{\mathfrak{r}}
as

(7.55)

z=F_{0}(w_{0}(z)-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})),\quad\text{for}\quad F_{0}:w\mapsto-t_{c}w+\frac{a}{3}w^{3}+\cal E(w).

Then, the implicit differentiation of (
7.55
) with respect to
w_{0}
and taking
z=\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)-t\widetilde{w}_{t}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t))
gives

\frac{1}{w_{0}^{\prime}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)-t\widetilde{w}_{t}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)))}=F^{\prime}_{0}\left(w_{0}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)-t\widetilde{w}_{t}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)))-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})\right).

In addition, taking
z=\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)-t\widetilde{w}_{t}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t))
in (
7.55
), we get that

(7.56)

\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)-t\widetilde{w}_{t}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t))=F_{0}(w_{0}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)-t\widetilde{w}_{t}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)))-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})).

Hence, (
7.54
) can be rewritten as the following equation of
w=w_{0}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)-t\widetilde{w}_{t}(\widetilde{E}_{\pm}(t)))-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})
:

(7.57)

aw^{2}=t_{c}-t+\OO\left(t^{2}+t|F_{0}(w)|+|w|^{3}\right)=t_{c}-t+\OO\left(t^{2}+|w|^{3}\right).

At the cusp
(\widetilde{x}_{c},\widetilde{t}_{c})
, we have
\widetilde{E}_{+}(\widetilde{t}_{c})=\widetilde{E}_{-}(\widetilde{t}_{c})
. Hence, the above equation (
7.57
) of
w
has a double root around 0 when
t=\widetilde{t}_{c}
, from which we readily get that
\widetilde{t}_{c}-t_{c}=\OO(\Delta t^{2})
.

For the estimate on
\widetilde{x}_{c}-x_{c}
, from (
7.57
), we can solve that
w_{0}(\widetilde{x}_{c}-\widetilde{t}_{c}\widetilde{w}_{\widetilde{t}_{c}}(\widetilde{x}_{c}))-w_{t_{c}}(x_{c})=\OO(\Delta t)
. Applying it to (
7.56
) and (
7.52
), we get that
